========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi. Um, thank you. I’m here because... well, it’s a little hard to explain, but I just really need some help figuring out what's going on in my head. I work as a computer systems analyst, and I actually finished my graduate degree not too long ago, so I’ve always been someone who relies on logic and fixing things. I used to be so on top of everything. But lately, I just... I can’t shake the feeling that work isn’t safe anymore. 

And it’s not just about the building or the office, it’s this heavy, constant weight that I’m somehow fundamentally damaged now. I sit at my desk, looking at my monitors and the projects I'm supposed to be managing, and I just feel like the competent, driven person who worked so hard and got promoted just... isn’t really there anymore. It’s like she’s completely gone, and I’m just this broken version trying to pretend I still know how to function. I keep trying to analyze my way out of it—because that’s what I do, I find the flaw in the system and I patch it—but no matter how much I try to reason with myself, I just have this overwhelming belief that I'm ruined and that nothing around me is secure. I don't know if that makes any sense to you, but I just keep spinning my wheels on this, and it's exhausting trying to hide the fact that I feel so entirely different inside.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I know exactly when it started, actually. It’s been... let’s see, about two years and four months now. I can pinpoint the exact timeline because it all traces back to this one specific project we were doing. In my line of work, when we have to push major system updates or do server migrations, we have to do overnight deployments. You know, you have to do it when all the users are offline so you don't disrupt the normal business hours or crash the network while people are trying to work. 

So, I was there late, doing this overnight deployment, just running my scripts and watching the progress bars on my monitors. It was supposed to be quiet, just me and the code, but... that's when the break-in happened. The building was broken into while I was there working. And ever since that specific night, two years and four months ago, this whole feeling just completely latched onto me. I keep looking at the calendar and calculating the time—like, twenty-eight months should be enough time to process an event logically, right? You'd think after more than two years you could just isolate the error, patch the bug, and move on. But ever since that break-in during the deployment, I just... I haven't been able to reset back to the person I was before that night. It's just been this continuous, unbroken loop since then.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, not really. I mean, I know what you're talking about, like a panic attack, right? I've read about those because, obviously, when my brain started glitching after the break-in, I started researching everything. I looked up anxiety, stress responses, the sympathetic nervous system, all of it. I wanted to understand the mechanics of what was happening to me. So I know the checklist—the hyperventilating, the feeling like you're having a heart attack, the sudden peak of terror out of nowhere. But I don't really get those. 

It's not like a sudden spike for me. It's more like... a constant hum in the background. Like a server that's running too hot, all the time. My heart doesn't suddenly start pounding out of my chest, and I don't start gasping for air or sweating through my clothes in the middle of a meeting. It's just this heavy, persistent dread. It's like I'm always waiting for the system to crash, rather than actually experiencing the crash itself, if that makes sense. I don't get those sudden, intense episodes where I feel like I'm dying or losing control in a matter of minutes. It's just a steady state of feeling unsafe.

It’s been... let’s see, about two years and four months now. I can pinpoint the exact timeline because it all traces back to this one specific project we were doing. In my line of work, when we have to push major system updates or do server migrations, we have to do overnight deployments. You know, you have to do it when all the users are offline so you don't disrupt the normal business hours or crash the network while people are trying to work. \n\nSo, I was there late, doing this overnight deployment, just running my scripts and watching the progress bars on my monitors. It was supposed to be quiet, just me and the code, but... that's when the break-in happened. The building was broken into while I was there working. And ever since that specific night, two years and four months ago, this whole feeling just completely latched onto me. I keep looking at the calendar and calculating the time—like, twenty-eight months should be enough time to process an event logically, right? You'd think after more than two years you could just isolate the error, patch the bug, and move on. But ever since that break-in during the deployment, I just... I haven't been able to reset back to the person I was before that night. It's just been this continuous, unbroken loop since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes where you felt an intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes, with symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or shortness of breath?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I know what you're talking about, like a panic attack, right? I've read about those because, obviously, when my brain started glitching after the break-in, I started researching everything. I looked up anxiety, stress responses, the sympathetic nervous system, all of it. I wanted to understand the mechanics of what was happening to me. So I know the checklist—the hyperventilating, the feeling like you're having a heart attack, the sudden peak of terror out of nowhere. But I don't really get those. \n\nIt's not like a sudden spike for me. It's more like... a constant hum in the background. Like a server that's running too hot, all the time. My heart doesn't suddenly start pounding out of my chest, and I don't start gasping for air or sweating through my clothes in the middle of a meeting. It's just this heavy, persistent dread. It's like I'm always waiting for the system to crash, rather than actually experiencing the crash itself, if that makes sense. I don't get those sudden, intense episodes where I feel like I'm dying or losing control in a matter of minutes. It's just a steady state of feeling unsafe.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Well, it definitely bleeds over, but it’s most obvious at work because that’s where the metrics are, you know? Like, I can track the decline. And my boss noticed. I actually got called out in my last performance review, which was devastating. I loved doing that, connecting with younger women entering the field, helping them navigate the corporate structure. But I’ve completely pulled back from all of it. I stepped down from the committee and I haven't taken on a new mentee in over a year. I just... I don’t feel steady anymore. I don't feel reliable. How can I sit there and give someone career advice or talk about leadership when I feel like my own internal architecture is collapsing? I used to be so on top of everything. But lately, I just... I can’t shake the feeling that work isn’t safe anymore. \n\nAnd it’s not just about the building or the office, it’s this heavy, constant weight that I’m somehow fundamentally damaged now. I sit at my desk, looking at my monitors and the projects I'm supposed to be managing, and I just feel like the competent, driven person who worked so hard and got promoted just... isn’t really there anymore. It’s like she’s completely gone, and I’m just this broken version trying to pretend I still know how to function. I keep trying to analyze my way out of it—because that’s what I do, I find the flaw in the system and I patch it—but no matter how much I try to reason with myself, I just have this overwhelming belief that I'm ruined and that nothing around me is secure. I don't know if that makes any sense to you, but I just keep spinning my wheels on this, and it's exhausting trying to hide the fact that I feel so entirely different inside.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Well, I know exactly when it started, actually. It’s been... let’s see, about two years and four months now. I can pinpoint the exact timeline because it all traces back to this one specific project we were doing. In my line of work, when we have to push major system updates or do server migrations, we have to do overnight deployments. You know, you have to do it when all the users are offline so you don't disrupt the normal business hours or crash the network while people are trying to work. \n\nSo, I was there late, doing this overnight deployment, just running my scripts and watching the progress bars on my monitors. It was supposed to be quiet, just me and the code, but... that's when the break-in happened. The building was broken into while I was there working. And ever since that specific night, two years and four months ago, this whole feeling just completely latched onto me. I keep looking at the calendar and calculating the time—like, twenty-eight months should be enough time to process an event logically, right? You'd think after more than two years you could just isolate the error, patch the bug, and move on. But ever since that break-in during the deployment, I just... I haven't been able to reset back to the person I was before that night. It's just been this continuous, unbroken loop since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes where you felt an intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes, with symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or shortness of breath?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I know what you're talking about, like a panic attack, right? I've read about those because, obviously, when my brain started glitching after the break-in, I started researching everything. I looked up anxiety, stress responses, the sympathetic nervous system, all of it. I wanted to understand the mechanics of what was happening to me. So I know the checklist—the hyperventilating, the feeling like you're having a heart attack, the sudden peak of terror out of nowhere. But I don't really get those. \n\nIt's not like a sudden spike for me. It's more like... a constant hum in the background. Like a server that's running too hot, all the time. My heart doesn't suddenly start pounding out of my chest, and I don't start gasping for air or sweating through my clothes in the middle of a meeting. It's just this heavy, persistent dread. It's like I'm always waiting for the system to crash, rather than actually experiencing the crash itself, if that makes sense. I don't get those sudden, intense episodes where I feel like I'm dying or losing control in a matter of minutes. It's just a steady state of feeling unsafe.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this sense of being damaged and unsafe only at work, or does it also affect how you feel in other parts of your life, like with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Well, it definitely bleeds over, but it’s most obvious at work because that’s where the metrics are, you know? Like, I can track the decline. It’s not just a feeling; it’s actually impacting my deliverables. My work has slipped enough that I’ve actually missed migration deadlines. And I never used to miss deadlines. I was the person who built the project timelines, so missing them is just... it’s completely unacceptable to me. But I’ve been actively avoiding key late deployments. If a script needs to be run at 2:00 AM, I’ll find an excuse to hand it off, or I’ll try to automate it in a way that’s riskier just so I don’t have to be the one monitoring it late at night. \n\nAnd my boss noticed. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yes. Yes, I do. And it’s actually become this whole... logistical nightmare, to be honest. I mean, my job is literally about optimizing systems and creating efficient workflows, but my own daily routine has become this incredibly convoluted, inefficient process just to avoid anything that triggers that feeling. 

For instance, the building itself. I used to just walk through the main lobby, take the elevator, normal stuff. But now, if I have to be there anywhere near the evening, I absolutely cannot use the main entrance. I’ve started taking this side entrance that goes through the old loading dock area, which is actually less secure logically, but for some reason, walking through that main lobby after dark just... it makes me feel like I’m going to come apart. Like my chest gets tight and I just start visualizing the glass breaking again. 

So I avoid the lobby. But it’s more than just the physical space, it’s the timing of the work itself. Like I mentioned before about the overnight deployments—I’ve been actively trading shifts with the other analysts. I’ll take their early morning database maintenance windows, which nobody wants because you have to log on at 4:30 AM, just so they’ll take my late-night release windows. And if I can’t trade, I’ve started pushing back on the release windows entirely. I’ll argue in the sprint planning meetings that we need to schedule the deployment for a weekend afternoon instead of a weeknight, citing user impact metrics that are, frankly, a bit of a stretch, just to justify not being in that building or even online late at night. 

And honestly, I’ve just been working from home as much as humanly possible. Our company has a hybrid policy, three days in, two days remote, but I’ve been stretching that to the absolute limit. I’ll claim I have a contractor coming, or I’m feeling slightly under the weather, or I need "deep focus time" for a complex script—anything to avoid going into the office, especially if I know I might have to stay past 5:00 PM. Because the second the sun starts going down and the office starts emptying out, that hum I told you about? It gets so loud I can't even look at my monitors. So yeah, I avoid it. I avoid the lobby, I avoid the late shifts, I avoid the building itself whenever I can. It's like I'm constantly rewriting my own schedule just to route around this massive error in my system.

It's just a steady state of feeling unsafe.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this sense of being damaged and unsafe only at work, or does it also affect how you feel in other parts of your life, like with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Well, it definitely bleeds over, but it’s most obvious at work because that’s where the metrics are, you know? Like, I can track the decline. It’s not just a feeling; it’s actually impacting my deliverables. My work has slipped enough that I’ve actually missed migration deadlines. And I never used to miss deadlines. I was the person who built the project timelines, so missing them is just... it’s completely unacceptable to me. But I’ve been actively avoiding key late deployments. If a script needs to be run at 2:00 AM, I’ll find an excuse to hand it off, or I’ll try to automate it in a way that’s riskier just so I don’t have to be the one monitoring it late at night. \n\nAnd my boss noticed. I actually got called out in my last performance review, which was devastating. It wasn't just the deadlines, either; they mentioned that I’ve been having conflicts with the team, getting irritable or snapping at people over code reviews or minor server configurations. And that never used to happen. I used to be the one who smoothed things over between the developers and the infrastructure guys. \n\nBut it’s not just the technical work, either. I used to be really involved in our mentorship program, and I was on the organizing committee for these women-in-tech leadership events in the city. I loved doing that, connecting with younger women entering the field, helping them navigate the corporate structure. But I’ve completely pulled back from all of it. I stepped down from the committee and I haven't taken on a new mentee in over a year. I just... I don’t feel steady anymore. I don't feel reliable. How can I sit there and give someone career advice or talk about leadership when I feel like my own internal architecture is collapsing? I just feel like a fraud trying to project this image of a successful, put-together analyst when, underneath it all, I'm dropping the ball on the most basic parts of my job.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid places or situations that remind you of the break-in at your workplace?\n\nPatient: Well, yes. Yes, I do. And it’s actually become this whole... logistical nightmare, to be honest. I mean, my job is literally about optimizing systems and creating efficient workflows, but my own daily routine has become this incredibly convoluted, inefficient process just to avoid anything that triggers that feeling. \n\nFor instance, the building itself. I used to just walk through the main lobby, take the elevator, normal stuff. But now, if I have to be there anywhere near the evening, I absolutely cannot use the main entrance. I’ve started taking this side entrance that goes through the old loading dock area, which is actually less secure logically, but for some reason, walking through that main lobby after dark just... it makes me feel like I’m going to come apart. Like my chest gets tight and I just start visualizing the glass breaking again. \n\nSo I avoid the lobby. But it’s more than just the physical space, it’s the timing of the work itself. Like I mentioned before about the overnight deployments—I’ve been actively trading shifts with the other analysts. I’ll take their early morning database maintenance windows, which nobody wants because you have to log on at 4:30 AM, just so they’ll take my late-night release windows. And if I can’t trade, I’ve started pushing back on the release windows entirely. I’ll argue in the sprint planning meetings that we need to schedule the deployment for a weekend afternoon instead of a weeknight, citing user impact metrics that are, frankly, a bit of a stretch, just to justify not being in that building or even online late at night. \n\nAnd honestly, I’ve just been working from home as much as humanly possible. Our company has a hybrid policy, three days in, two days remote, but I’ve been stretching that to the absolute limit. I’ll claim I have a contractor coming, or I’m feeling slightly under the weather, or I need \"deep focus time\" for a complex script—anything to avoid going into the office, especially if I know I might have to stay past 5:00 PM. Because the second the sun starts going down and the office starts emptying out, that hum I told you about? It gets so loud I can't even look at my monitors. So yeah, I avoid it. I avoid the lobby, I avoid the late shifts, I avoid the building itself whenever I can. It's like I'm constantly rewriting my own schedule just to route around this massive error in my system.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I’ll argue in the sprint planning meetings that we need to schedule the deployment for a weekend afternoon instead of a weeknight, citing user impact metrics that are, frankly, a bit of a stretch, just to justify not being in that building or even online late at night. \n\nAnd honestly, I’ve just been working from home as much as humanly possible. Our company has a hybrid policy, three days in, two days remote, but I’ve been stretching that to the absolute limit. I’ll claim I have a contractor coming, or I’m feeling slightly under the weather, or I need \"deep focus time\" for a complex script—anything to avoid going into the office, especially if I know I might have to stay past 5:00 PM. Because the second the sun starts going down and the office starts emptying out, that hum I told you about? It gets so loud I can't even look at my monitors. So yeah, I avoid it. I avoid the lobby, I avoid the late shifts, I avoid the building itself whenever I can. It's like I'm constantly rewriting my own schedule just to route around this massive error in my system.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often find yourself remembering or reliving the break-in at your workplace?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 28 / computer_systems_analyst / graduate\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Intrusive_Symptoms\nmanifestation: \"A badge scanner beeping, heavy footsteps in the hallway, or a late-night deployment alert can suddenly put me right back in that office, with my chest tight and the image of the guard on the floor cutting through whatever I’m doing.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah... yeah, I do. And it’s not even like I’m sitting there trying to process the event or think about it on purpose. It’s more like... well, in my line of work, we talk about system interrupts, right? Like a hardware interrupt that just halts the processor and forces it to deal with a high-priority event immediately. That’s exactly what it feels like. It’s entirely involuntary. 

It’s usually triggered by these really specific, otherwise completely mundane sensory inputs. Like... a badge scanner beeping. We have these RFID readers at the office, and they make this very distinct, sharp little chirp when someone badges in. Or, um, heavy footsteps in the hallway. You know how acoustics change when an office is mostly empty? The sound just carries differently, it echoes more. Or even something digital, like a late-night deployment alert pinging on my phone for a server update. 

When one of those things happens, it doesn't matter where I am or what I'm focusing on. It’s a total system override. I am suddenly put right back in that office on that night. It’s so incredibly visceral. My chest gets really tight, like I physically cannot draw a full breath, and... and then there's this image. The image of the security guard on the floor. It just violently cuts through whatever I’m doing. 

I could be mid-sentence in a team stand-up meeting, or deep into writing a complex block of code, or just standing in my kitchen making coffee, and suddenly I’m not there anymore. I’m right back in that moment, seeing him on the floor, hearing the sounds. And it takes me... I don't know, it takes a really long time to kind of reboot and convince my brain that I'm actually safe, that the event isn't happening right this second. It's just so exhausting because I can't control when someone is going to walk heavily down a hall or when a scanner is going to beep, so I'm just constantly vulnerable to my brain forcing me to relive the worst night of my life over and over again.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Um, thank you. I’m here because... well, it’s a little hard to explain, but I just really need some help figuring out what's going on in my head. I can’t shake the feeling that work isn’t safe anymore. \n\nAnd it’s not just about the building or the office, it’s this heavy, constant weight that I’m somehow fundamentally damaged now. I sit at my desk, looking at my monitors and the projects I'm supposed to be managing, and I just feel like the competent, driven person who worked so hard and got promoted just... isn’t really there anymore. It’s like she’s completely gone, and I’m just this broken version trying to pretend I still know how to function. I keep trying to analyze my way out of it—because that’s what I do, I find the flaw in the system and I patch it—but no matter how much I try to reason with myself, I just have this overwhelming belief that I'm ruined and that nothing around me is secure. I don't know if that makes any sense to you, but I just keep spinning my wheels on this, and it's exhausting trying to hide the fact that I feel so entirely different inside.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Well, I know exactly when it started, actually. It’s been... let’s see, about two years and four months now. I can pinpoint the exact timeline because it all traces back to this one specific project we were doing. In my line of work, when we have to push major system updates or do server migrations, we have to do overnight deployments. You know, you have to do it when all the users are offline so you don't disrupt the normal business hours or crash the network while people are trying to work. \n\nSo, I was there late, doing this overnight deployment, just running my scripts and watching the progress bars on my monitors. It was supposed to be quiet, just me and the code, but... that's when the break-in happened. The building was broken into while I was there working. And ever since that specific night, two years and four months ago, this whole feeling just completely latched onto me. I keep looking at the calendar and calculating the time—like, twenty-eight months should be enough time to process an event logically, right? You'd think after more than two years you could just isolate the error, patch the bug, and move on. But ever since that break-in during the deployment, I just... I haven't been able to reset back to the person I was before that night. It's just been this continuous, unbroken loop since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes where you felt an intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes, with symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or shortness of breath?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I know what you're talking about, like a panic attack, right? I've read about those because, obviously, when my brain started glitching after the break-in, I started researching everything. I looked up anxiety, stress responses, the sympathetic nervous system, all of it. I wanted to understand the mechanics of what was happening to me. So I know the checklist—the hyperventilating, the feeling like you're having a heart attack, the sudden peak of terror out of nowhere. But I don't really get those. \n\nIt's not like a sudden spike for me. It's more like... a constant hum in the background. Like a server that's running too hot, all the time. My heart doesn't suddenly start pounding out of my chest, and I don't start gasping for air or sweating through my clothes in the middle of a meeting. It's just this heavy, persistent dread. It's like I'm always waiting for the system to crash, rather than actually experiencing the crash itself, if that makes sense. I don't get those sudden, intense episodes where I feel like I'm dying or losing control in a matter of minutes. It's just a steady state of feeling unsafe.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this sense of being damaged and unsafe only at work, or does it also affect how you feel in other parts of your life, like with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Well, it definitely bleeds over, but it’s most obvious at work because that’s where the metrics are, you know? Like, I can track the decline. It’s not just a feeling; it’s actually impacting my deliverables. My work has slipped enough that I’ve actually missed migration deadlines. And I never used to miss deadlines. I was the person who built the project timelines, so missing them is just... it’s completely unacceptable to me. But I’ve been actively avoiding key late deployments. If a script needs to be run at 2:00 AM, I’ll find an excuse to hand it off, or I’ll try to automate it in a way that’s riskier just so I don’t have to be the one monitoring it late at night. \n\nAnd my boss noticed. I actually got called out in my last performance review, which was devastating. I used to be really involved in our mentorship program, and I was on the organizing committee for these women-in-tech leadership events in the city. I loved doing that, connecting with younger women entering the field, helping them navigate the corporate structure. But I’ve completely pulled back from all of it. I stepped down from the committee and I haven't taken on a new mentee in over a year. I just... I don’t feel steady anymore. I don't feel reliable. How can I sit there and give someone career advice or talk about leadership when I feel like my own internal architecture is collapsing? I just feel like a fraud trying to project this image of a successful, put-together analyst when, underneath it all, I'm dropping the ball on the most basic parts of my job.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid places or situations that remind you of the break-in at your workplace?\n\nPatient: Well, yes. manifestation: I remember hiding and I remember the police being there, but there’s this blank section around when they actually came into the conference room and got me out, like the file is corrupted no matter how hard I try to open it.\n- key: S072\n  name: Negative_Beliefs\n  description: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.\n  manifestation: I’m here because I can’t shake the feeling that work isn’t safe anymore and that I’m somehow damaged now, like the competent person who got promoted isn’t really there anymore.\n- key: S073\n  name: Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame\n  description: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.\n  manifestation: I keep thinking that if I had insisted on better security for the overnight deployment or called sooner, the guard might not have been hurt so badly, even though part of me knows I was hiding and terrified.\n- key: S077\n  name: Irritability_Aggression\n  description: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.\n  manifestation: In code reviews I snap at people over small things, like a sloppy rollback plan or someone questioning my timeline, and then I hear myself sounding harsh and personal before I can pull it back.\n- key: S079\n  name: Hypervigilance\n  description: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.\n  manifestation: On the train, in meetings, even at lunch with my team, I automatically clock the exits, watch who is standing too close, and keep one ear tuned to hallway noises instead of being fully present.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: My work has slipped enough that I’ve missed migration deadlines, avoided key late deployments, and gotten called out in a performance review for conflicts that never used to happen. I’ve also pulled back from mentoring and women-in-tech leadership events because I don’t feel steady or reliable there anymore.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 years and 4 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on since the break-in during that overnight deployment about 2 years and 4 months ago.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 28 / computer_systems_analyst / graduate\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S072\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you feel this sense of being damaged and unsafe only at work, or does it also affect how you feel in other parts of your life, like with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Well, it definitely bleeds over, but it’s most obvious at work because that’s where the metrics are, you know? Like, I can track the decline. It’s not just a feeling; it’s actually impacting my deliverables. My work has slipped enough that I’ve actually missed migration deadlines. And I never used to miss deadlines. I was the person who built the project timelines, so missing them is just... it’s completely unacceptable to me. But I’ve been actively avoiding key late deployments. If a script needs to be run at 2:00 AM, I’ll find an excuse to hand it off, or I’ll try to automate it in a way that’s riskier just so I don’t have to be the one monitoring it late at night. \n\nAnd my boss noticed. I actually got called out in my last performance review, which was devastating. It wasn't just the deadlines, either; they mentioned that I’ve been having conflicts with the team, getting irritable or snapping at people over code reviews or minor server configurations. And that never used to happen. I used to be the one who smoothed things over between the developers and the infrastructure guys. \n\nBut it’s not just the technical work, either. I used to be really involved in our mentorship program, and I was on the organizing committee for these women-in-tech leadership events in the city. I loved doing that, connecting with younger women entering the field, helping them navigate the corporate structure. But I’ve completely pulled back from all of it. I stepped down from the committee and I haven't taken on a new mentee in over a year. I just... I don’t feel steady anymore. I don't feel reliable. How can I sit there and give someone career advice or talk about leadership when I feel like my own internal architecture is collapsing? I just feel like a fraud trying to project this image of a successful, put-together analyst when, underneath it all, I'm dropping the ball on the most basic parts of my job.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid places or situations that remind you of the break-in at your workplace?\n\nPatient: Well, yes. Yes, I do. And it’s actually become this whole... logistical nightmare, to be honest. I mean, my job is literally about optimizing systems and creating efficient workflows, but my own daily routine has become this incredibly convoluted, inefficient process just to avoid anything that triggers that feeling. \n\nFor instance, the building itself. I used to just walk through the main lobby, take the elevator, normal stuff. But now, if I have to be there anywhere near the evening, I absolutely cannot use the main entrance. I’ve started taking this side entrance that goes through the old loading dock area, which is actually less secure logically, but for some reason, walking through that main lobby after dark just... it makes me feel like I’m going to come apart. Like my chest gets tight and I just start visualizing the glass breaking again. \n\nSo I avoid the lobby. But it’s more than just the physical space, it’s the timing of the work itself. Like I mentioned before about the overnight deployments—I’ve been actively trading shifts with the other analysts. I’ll take their early morning database maintenance windows, which nobody wants because you have to log on at 4:30 AM, just so they’ll take my late-night release windows. And if I can’t trade, I’ve started pushing back on the release windows entirely. I’ll argue in the sprint planning meetings that we need to schedule the deployment for a weekend afternoon instead of a weeknight, citing user impact metrics that are, frankly, a bit of a stretch, just to justify not being in that building or even online late at night. \n\nAnd honestly, I’ve just been working from home as much as humanly possible. Our company has a hybrid policy, three days in, two days remote, but I’ve been stretching that to the absolute limit. I’ll claim I have a contractor coming, or I’m feeling slightly under the weather, or I need \"deep focus time\" for a complex script—anything to avoid going into the office, especially if I know I might have to stay past 5:00 PM. Because the second the sun starts going down and the office starts emptying out, that hum I told you about? It gets so loud I can't even look at my monitors. So yeah, I avoid it. I avoid the lobby, I avoid the late shifts, I avoid the building itself whenever I can. It's like I'm constantly rewriting my own schedule just to route around this massive error in my system.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself remembering or reliving the break-in at your workplace?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, I do. And it’s not even like I’m sitting there trying to process the event or think about it on purpose. It’s more like... well, in my line of work, we talk about system interrupts, right? Like a hardware interrupt that just halts the processor and forces it to deal with a high-priority event immediately. That’s exactly what it feels like. It’s entirely involuntary. \n\nIt’s usually triggered by these really specific, otherwise completely mundane sensory inputs. Like... a badge scanner beeping. We have these RFID readers at the office, and they make this very distinct, sharp little chirp when someone badges in. Or, um, heavy footsteps in the hallway. You know how acoustics change when an office is mostly empty? The sound just carries differently, it echoes more. Or even something digital, like a late-night deployment alert pinging on my phone for a server update. \n\nWhen one of those things happens, it doesn't matter where I am or what I'm focusing on. It’s a total system override. I am suddenly put right back in that office on that night. It’s so incredibly visceral. My chest gets really tight, like I physically cannot draw a full breath, and... and then there's this image. The image of the security guard on the floor. It just violently cuts through whatever I’m doing. \n\nI could be mid-sentence in a team stand-up meeting, or deep into writing a complex block of code, or just standing in my kitchen making coffee, and suddenly I’m not there anymore. I’m right back in that moment, seeing him on the floor, hearing the sounds. And it takes me... I don't know, it takes a really long time to kind of reboot and convince my brain that I'm actually safe, that the event isn't happening right this second. It's just so exhausting because I can't control when someone is going to walk heavily down a hall or when a scanner is going to beep, so I'm just constantly vulnerable to my brain forcing me to relive the worst night of my life over and over again.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever feel like you're observing yourself from outside your body or like you're in a dream when you're reliving the break-in?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

No, I... I don't think so. I mean, I know what you're talking about, I think. I read an article once about depersonalization, how the brain sort of detaches as a defense mechanism during trauma. Like you're watching a movie of yourself. But no, that's not what happens to me at all. It's actually the exact opposite. 

When I get pulled back into that memory, it's not like I'm floating above it or watching it happen to someone else. I am completely, horribly present in my own body. It's so visceral. Every physical sensation is magnified. The tightness in my chest, the way my heart starts hammering against my ribs, the sweat on my palms... it's all incredibly immediate. I'm not disconnected from it; I'm trapped in it. 

I almost wish I could disconnect, honestly. If I could just float away and watch it like a movie, maybe it wouldn't be so exhausting. But instead, it's like my nervous system is firing on all cylinders, screaming that the threat is right in front of me, right now. It's so grounding, but in the worst possible way. It anchors me to that panic. So, no, I don't feel like I'm in a dream or outside myself. I feel entirely too real, and entirely too stuck in that exact physical moment.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Um, thank you. I’m here because... well, it’s a little hard to explain, but I just really need some help figuring out what's going on in my head. I keep trying to analyze my way out of it—because that’s what I do, I find the flaw in the system and I patch it—but no matter how much I try to reason with myself, I just have this overwhelming belief that I'm ruined and that nothing around me is secure. I don't know if that makes any sense to you, but I just keep spinning my wheels on this, and it's exhausting trying to hide the fact that I feel so entirely different inside.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Well, I know exactly when it started, actually. It's just so exhausting because I can't control when someone is going to walk heavily down a hall or when a scanner is going to beep, so I'm just constantly vulnerable to my brain forcing me to relive the worst night of my life over and over again.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're observing yourself from outside your body or like you're in a dream when you're reliving the break-in?\n\nPatient: No, I... I don't think so. I mean, I know what you're talking about, I think. I read an article once about depersonalization, how the brain sort of detaches as a defense mechanism during trauma. Like you're watching a movie of yourself. But no, that's not what happens to me at all. It's actually the exact opposite. \n\nWhen I get pulled back into that memory, it's not like I'm floating above it or watching it happen to someone else. I am completely, horribly present in my own body. It's so visceral. Every physical sensation is magnified. The tightness in my chest, the way my heart starts hammering against my ribs, the sweat on my palms... it's all incredibly immediate. I'm not disconnected from it; I'm trapped in it. \n\nI almost wish I could disconnect, honestly. If I could just float away and watch it like a movie, maybe it wouldn't be so exhausting. But instead, it's like my nervous system is firing on all cylinders, screaming that the threat is right in front of me, right now. It's so grounding, but in the worst possible way. It anchors me to that panic. So, no, I don't feel like I'm in a dream or outside myself. I feel entirely too real, and entirely too stuck in that exact physical moment.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I just feel like a fraud trying to project this image of a successful, put-together analyst when, underneath it all, I'm dropping the ball on the most basic parts of my job.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid places or situations that remind you of the break-in at your workplace?\n\nPatient: Well, yes. Yes, I do. And it’s actually become this whole... logistical nightmare, to be honest. It just violently cuts through whatever I’m doing. \n\nI could be mid-sentence in a team stand-up meeting, or deep into writing a complex block of code, or just standing in my kitchen making coffee, and suddenly I’m not there anymore. I’m right back in that moment, seeing him on the floor, hearing the sounds. And it takes me... I don't know, it takes a really long time to kind of reboot and convince my brain that I'm actually safe, that the event isn't happening right this second. It's just so exhausting because I can't control when someone is going to walk heavily down a hall or when a scanner is going to beep, so I'm just constantly vulnerable to my brain forcing me to relive the worst night of my life over and over again.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're observing yourself from outside your body or like you're in a dream when you're reliving the break-in?\n\nPatient: No, I... I don't think so. I mean, I know what you're talking about, I think. I read an article once about depersonalization, how the brain sort of detaches as a defense mechanism during trauma. Like you're watching a movie of yourself. But no, that's not what happens to me at all. It's actually the exact opposite. \n\nWhen I get pulled back into that memory, it's not like I'm floating above it or watching it happen to someone else. I am completely, horribly present in my own body. It's so visceral. Every physical sensation is magnified. The tightness in my chest, the way my heart starts hammering against my ribs, the sweat on my palms... it's all incredibly immediate. I'm not disconnected from it; I'm trapped in it. \n\nI almost wish I could disconnect, honestly. If I could just float away and watch it like a movie, maybe it wouldn't be so exhausting. But instead, it's like my nervous system is firing on all cylinders, screaming that the threat is right in front of me, right now. It's so grounding, but in the worst possible way. It anchors me to that panic. So, no, I don't feel like I'm in a dream or outside myself. I feel entirely too real, and entirely too stuck in that exact physical moment.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you try to avoid thinking or talking about the break-in at your workplace?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 28 / computer_systems_analyst / graduate\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I... I don't know if "try" is the right word, because honestly, I don't really have to try. I just... don't. I mean, I don't go out of my way to bring it up, obviously. If you're asking if I actively suppress thoughts about it, it's hard to say. I guess I just try to keep my mind occupied with other things. 

Like, at work, I'll dive into a really complex data migration plan, or I'll spend hours debugging a script that probably doesn't even need that much attention, just to keep my processing power maxed out, you know? If my brain is running at 100% capacity on a logic problem, there's no bandwidth left for... for those memories to load. 

And as for talking about it, well, who am I going to talk to? My coworkers? They were either there and don't want to talk about it either, or they weren't and they just look at me with this... pity. I hate that look. My friends try, but they don't get it. They say things like, "At least you weren't hurt," which, logically, yes, is true, but it completely misses the point of how it fundamentally altered my baseline operating state. So, I just don't bring it up. It's not like I have a sticky note on my monitor that says "Don't think about the break-in," but I definitely don't invite the topic into my daily stand-ups or my personal life. It's just... easier to keep that file closed, if that makes sense.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Um, thank you. I’m here because... well, it’s a little hard to explain, but I just really need some help figuring out what's going on in my head. I work as a computer systems analyst, and I actually finished my graduate degree not too long ago, so I’ve always been someone who relies on logic and fixing things. I used to be so on top of everything. But lately, I just... I can’t shake the feeling that work isn’t safe anymore. \n\nAnd it’s not just about the building or the office, it’s this heavy, constant weight that I’m somehow fundamentally damaged now. I sit at my desk, looking at my monitors and the projects I'm supposed to be managing, and I just feel like the competent, driven person who worked so hard and got promoted just... isn’t really there anymore. It’s like she’s completely gone, and I’m just this broken version trying to pretend I still know how to function. I keep trying to analyze my way out of it—because that’s what I do, I find the flaw in the system and I patch it—but no matter how much I try to reason with myself, I just have this overwhelming belief that I'm ruined and that nothing around me is secure. I don't know if that makes any sense to you, but I just keep spinning my wheels on this, and it's exhausting trying to hide the fact that I feel so entirely different inside.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Well, I know exactly when it started, actually. It’s been... let’s see, about two years and four months now. I can pinpoint the exact timeline because it all traces back to this one specific project we were doing. In my line of work, when we have to push major system updates or do server migrations, we have to do overnight deployments. You know, you have to do it when all the users are offline so you don't disrupt the normal business hours or crash the network while people are trying to work. \n\nSo, I was there late, doing this overnight deployment, just running my scripts and watching the progress bars on my monitors. It was supposed to be quiet, just me and the code, but... that's when the break-in happened. The building was broken into while I was there working. And ever since that specific night, two years and four months ago, this whole feeling just completely latched onto me. I keep looking at the calendar and calculating the time—like, twenty-eight months should be enough time to process an event logically, right? You'd think after more than two years you could just isolate the error, patch the bug, and move on. But ever since that break-in during the deployment, I just... I haven't been able to reset back to the person I was before that night. It's just been this continuous, unbroken loop since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes where you felt an intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes, with symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or shortness of breath?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I know what you're talking about, like a panic attack, right? I've read about those because, obviously, when my brain started glitching after the break-in, I started researching everything. I looked up anxiety, stress responses, the sympathetic nervous system, all of it. I wanted to understand the mechanics of what was happening to me. So I know the checklist—the hyperventilating, the feeling like you're having a heart attack, the sudden peak of terror out of nowhere. But I don't really get those. \n\nIt's not like a sudden spike for me. It's more like... a constant hum in the background. Like a server that's running too hot, all the time. My heart doesn't suddenly start pounding out of my chest, and I don't start gasping for air or sweating through my clothes in the middle of a meeting. It's just this heavy, persistent dread. It's like I'm always waiting for the system to crash, rather than actually experiencing the crash itself, if that makes sense. I don't get those sudden, intense episodes where I feel like I'm dying or losing control in a matter of minutes. It's just a steady state of feeling unsafe.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this sense of being damaged and unsafe only at work, or does it also affect how you feel in other parts of your life, like with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Well, it definitely bleeds over, but it’s most obvious at work because that’s where the metrics are, you know? Like, I can track the decline. It’s not just a feeling; it’s actually impacting my deliverables. My work has slipped enough that I’ve actually missed migration deadlines. And I never used to miss deadlines. I was the person who built the project timelines, so missing them is just... it’s completely unacceptable to me. But I’ve been actively avoiding key late deployments. If a script needs to be run at 2:00 AM, I’ll find an excuse to hand it off, or I’ll try to automate it in a way that’s riskier just so I don’t have to be the one monitoring it late at night. \n\nAnd my boss noticed. I actually got called out in my last performance review, which was devastating. It wasn't just the deadlines, either; they mentioned that I’ve been having conflicts with the team, getting irritable or snapping at people over code reviews or minor server configurations. And that never used to happen. I used to be the one who smoothed things over between the developers and the infrastructure guys. \n\nBut it’s not just the technical work, either. I used to be really involved in our mentorship program, and I was on the organizing committee for these women-in-tech leadership events in the city. I loved doing that, connecting with younger women entering the field, helping them navigate the corporate structure. But I’ve completely pulled back from all of it. I stepped down from the committee and I haven't taken on a new mentee in over a year. I just... I don’t feel steady anymore. I don't feel reliable. How can I sit there and give someone career advice or talk about leadership when I feel like my own internal architecture is collapsing? I just feel like a fraud trying to project this image of a successful, put-together analyst when, underneath it all, I'm dropping the ball on the most basic parts of my job.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid places or situations that remind you of the break-in at your workplace?\n\nPatient: Well, yes. Yes, I do. And it’s actually become this whole... logistical nightmare, to be honest. I mean, my job is literally about optimizing systems and creating efficient workflows, but my own daily routine has become this incredibly convoluted, inefficient process just to avoid anything that triggers that feeling. \n\nFor instance, the building itself. I used to just walk through the main lobby, take the elevator, normal stuff. But now, if I have to be there anywhere near the evening, I absolutely cannot use the main entrance. I’ve started taking this side entrance that goes through the old loading dock area, which is actually less secure logically, but for some reason, walking through that main lobby after dark just... it makes me feel like I’m going to come apart. Like my chest gets tight and I just start visualizing the glass breaking again. \n\nSo I avoid the lobby. But it’s more than just the physical space, it’s the timing of the work itself. Like I mentioned before about the overnight deployments—I’ve been actively trading shifts with the other analysts. I’ll take their early morning database maintenance windows, which nobody wants because you have to log on at 4:30 AM, just so they’ll take my late-night release windows. And if I can’t trade, I’ve started pushing back on the release windows entirely. I’ll argue in the sprint planning meetings that we need to schedule the deployment for a weekend afternoon instead of a weeknight, citing user impact metrics that are, frankly, a bit of a stretch, just to justify not being in that building or even online late at night. \n\nAnd honestly, I’ve just been working from home as much as humanly possible. Our company has a hybrid policy, three days in, two days remote, but I’ve been stretching that to the absolute limit. I’ll claim I have a contractor coming, or I’m feeling slightly under the weather, or I need \"deep focus time\" for a complex script—anything to avoid going into the office, especially if I know I might have to stay past 5:00 PM. Because the second the sun starts going down and the office starts emptying out, that hum I told you about? It gets so loud I can't even look at my monitors. So yeah, I avoid it. I avoid the lobby, I avoid the late shifts, I avoid the building itself whenever I can. It's like I'm constantly rewriting my own schedule just to route around this massive error in my system.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself remembering or reliving the break-in at your workplace?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, I do. And it’s not even like I’m sitting there trying to process the event or think about it on purpose. It’s more like... well, in my line of work, we talk about system interrupts, right? Like a hardware interrupt that just halts the processor and forces it to deal with a high-priority event immediately. That’s exactly what it feels like. It’s entirely involuntary. \n\nIt’s usually triggered by these really specific, otherwise completely mundane sensory inputs. Like... a badge scanner beeping. We have these RFID readers at the office, and they make this very distinct, sharp little chirp when someone badges in. Or, um, heavy footsteps in the hallway. You know how acoustics change when an office is mostly empty? The sound just carries differently, it echoes more. Or even something digital, like a late-night deployment alert pinging on my phone for a server update. \n\nWhen one of those things happens, it doesn't matter where I am or what I'm focusing on. It’s a total system override. I am suddenly put right back in that office on that night. It’s so incredibly visceral. My chest gets really tight, like I physically cannot draw a full breath, and... and then there's this image. The image of the security guard on the floor. It just violently cuts through whatever I’m doing. \n\nI could be mid-sentence in a team stand-up meeting, or deep into writing a complex block of code, or just standing in my kitchen making coffee, and suddenly I’m not there anymore. I’m right back in that moment, seeing him on the floor, hearing the sounds. And it takes me... I don't know, it takes a really long time to kind of reboot and convince my brain that I'm actually safe, that the event isn't happening right this second. It's just so exhausting because I can't control when someone is going to walk heavily down a hall or when a scanner is going to beep, so I'm just constantly vulnerable to my brain forcing me to relive the worst night of my life over and over again.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're observing yourself from outside your body or like you're in a dream when you're reliving the break-in?\n\nPatient: No, I... I don't think so. I mean, I know what you're talking about, I think. I read an article once about depersonalization, how the brain sort of detaches as a defense mechanism during trauma. It's actually the exact opposite. \n\nWhen I get pulled back into that memory, it's not like I'm floating above it or watching it happen to someone else. I am completely, horribly present in my own body. It's so visceral. Every physical sensation is magnified. The tightness in my chest, the way my heart starts hammering against my ribs, the sweat on my palms... it's all incredibly immediate. I'm not disconnected from it; I'm trapped in it. \n\nI almost wish I could disconnect, honestly. If I could just float away and watch it like a movie, maybe it wouldn't be so exhausting. But instead, it's like my nervous system is firing on all cylinders, screaming that the threat is right in front of me, right now. It's so grounding, but in the worst possible way. It anchors me to that panic. So, no, I don't feel like I'm in a dream or outside myself. I feel entirely too real, and entirely too stuck in that exact physical moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to avoid thinking or talking about the break-in at your workplace?\n\nPatient: Well, I... They say things like, \"At least you weren't hurt,\" which, logically, yes, is true, but it completely misses the point of how it fundamentally altered my baseline operating state. So, I just don't bring it up. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yes. Yes, I have. It’s... it’s actually been bleeding into everything, which is incredibly frustrating because I usually pride myself on compartmentalizing. But lately, it’s like my whole system architecture is just degrading. 

At work, my performance has definitely slipped, and that’s hard for me to even admit out loud. I’ve always been the one people rely on to hit the mark, but recently, I’ve actually missed a couple of major data migration deadlines. I just... I get so caught up in trying to manage my own baseline anxiety, trying to keep those intrusive memories at bay, that my focus just shatters. And I’ve started actively avoiding key late-night deployments, which is a huge part of a systems analyst role. But being online late, seeing those alerts ping... it’s just too much of a trigger right now, so I make excuses or try to hand them off. 

It’s gotten to the point where my manager actually brought it up in my last performance review. Not just the missed deadlines, but my attitude. Apparently, I’ve been getting into conflicts with my team, snapping at people over code reviews or system architecture disagreements. And that never used to happen. I was always the mediator, the calm one. But now, my fuse is just so short. I feel like I'm constantly running on empty, so any little friction just sparks a massive reaction. 

And outside of my direct job duties, it’s affecting the things I actually used to care about. I’ve completely pulled back from mentoring the junior analysts. I used to love doing that, helping them navigate the tech stack and the corporate environment. I was also really involved in these women-in-tech leadership events in the city—organizing panels, networking, speaking. But I haven't gone to a single one in months. I just... I don't feel steady anymore. I don't feel reliable. How can I stand up there and talk about leadership and resilience when I can barely get through a Tuesday without feeling like I'm going to jump out of my skin? So, I just isolate. I stay home. It’s safer, but it’s really lonely, and it feels like I’m losing all these parts of my identity that I worked so hard to build.

I don't know if that makes any sense to you, but I just keep spinning my wheels on this, and it's exhausting trying to hide the fact that I feel so entirely different inside.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Well, I know exactly when it started, actually. It’s been... let’s see, about two years and four months now. I can pinpoint the exact timeline because it all traces back to this one specific project we were doing. In my line of work, when we have to push major system updates or do server migrations, we have to do overnight deployments.
