It was supposed to be quiet, just me and the code, but... that's when the break-in happened. The building was broken into while I was there working. And ever since that specific night, two years and four months ago, this whole feeling just completely latched onto me. I keep looking at the calendar and calculating the time—like, twenty-eight months should be enough time to process an event logically, right? You'd think after more than two years you could just isolate the error, patch the bug, and move on. But ever since that break-in during the deployment, I just... I haven't been able to reset back to the person I was before that night. It's just been this continuous, unbroken loop since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes where you felt an intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes, with symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or shortness of breath?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I know what you're talking about, like a panic attack, right? I've read about those because, obviously, when my brain started glitching after the break-in, I started researching everything. I looked up anxiety, stress responses, the sympathetic nervous system, all of it. I wanted to understand the mechanics of what was happening to me. So I know the checklist—the hyperventilating, the feeling like you're having a heart attack, the sudden peak of terror out of nowhere. But I don't really get those. \n\nIt's not like a sudden spike for me. It's more like... a constant hum in the background. Like a server that's running too hot, all the time. My heart doesn't suddenly start pounding out of my chest, and I don't start gasping for air or sweating through my clothes in the middle of a meeting. It's just this heavy, persistent dread. It's like I'm always waiting for the system to crash, rather than actually experiencing the crash itself, if that makes sense. I don't get those sudden, intense episodes where I feel like I'm dying or losing control in a matter of minutes. It's just a steady state of feeling unsafe.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this sense of being damaged and unsafe only at work, or does it also affect how you feel in other parts of your life, like with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Well, it definitely bleeds over, but it’s most obvious at work because that’s where the metrics are, you know? Like, I can track the decline. It’s not just a feeling; it’s actually impacting my deliverables. My work has slipped enough that I’ve actually missed migration deadlines. And I never used to miss deadlines. I was the person who built the project timelines, so missing them is just... it’s completely unacceptable to me. But I’ve been actively avoiding key late deployments. If a script needs to be run at 2:00 AM, I’ll find an excuse to hand it off, or I’ll try to automate it in a way that’s riskier just so I don’t have to be the one monitoring it late at night. \n\nAnd my boss noticed. I actually got called out in my last performance review, which was devastating. It wasn't just the deadlines, either; they mentioned that I’ve been having conflicts with the team, getting irritable or snapping at people over code reviews or minor server configurations. And that never used to happen. I used to be the one who smoothed things over between the developers and the infrastructure guys. \n\nBut it’s not just the technical work, either. I used to be really involved in our mentorship program, and I was on the organizing committee for these women-in-tech leadership events in the city. I loved doing that, connecting with younger women entering the field, helping them navigate the corporate structure. But I’ve completely pulled back from all of it. I stepped down from the committee and I haven't taken on a new mentee in over a year. I just... I don’t feel steady anymore. I don't feel reliable. How can I sit there and give someone career advice or talk about leadership when I feel like my own internal architecture is collapsing? I just feel like a fraud trying to project this image of a successful, put-together analyst when, underneath it all, I'm dropping the ball on the most basic parts of my job.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid places or situations that remind you of the break-in at your workplace?\n\nPatient: Well, yes. Yes, I do. And it’s actually become this whole... logistical nightmare, to be honest. I mean, my job is literally about optimizing systems and creating efficient workflows, but my own daily routine has become this incredibly convoluted, inefficient process just to avoid anything that triggers that feeling. \n\nFor instance, the building itself. I used to just walk through the main lobby, take the elevator, normal stuff. But now, if I have to be there anywhere near the evening, I absolutely cannot use the main entrance. I’ve started taking this side entrance that goes through the old loading dock area, which is actually less secure logically, but for some reason, walking through that main lobby after dark just... it makes me feel like I’m going to come apart. Like my chest gets tight and I just start visualizing the glass breaking again. \n\nSo I avoid the lobby. But it’s more than just the physical space, it’s the timing of the work itself. Like I mentioned before about the overnight deployments—I’ve been actively trading shifts with the other analysts. I’ll take their early morning database maintenance windows, which nobody wants because you have to log on at 4:30 AM, just so they’ll take my late-night release windows. And if I can’t trade, I’ve started pushing back on the release windows entirely. I’ll argue in the sprint planning meetings that we need to schedule the deployment for a weekend afternoon instead of a weeknight, citing user impact metrics that are, frankly, a bit of a stretch, just to justify not being in that building or even online late at night. \n\nAnd honestly, I’ve just been working from home as much as humanly possible. Our company has a hybrid policy, three days in, two days remote, but I’ve been stretching that to the absolute limit. I’ll claim I have a contractor coming, or I’m feeling slightly under the weather, or I need \"deep focus time\" for a complex script—anything to avoid going into the office, especially if I know I might have to stay past 5:00 PM. Because the second the sun starts going down and the office starts emptying out, that hum I told you about? It gets so loud I can't even look at my monitors. So yeah, I avoid it. I avoid the lobby, I avoid the late shifts, I avoid the building itself whenever I can. It's like I'm constantly rewriting my own schedule just to route around this massive error in my system.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself remembering or reliving the break-in at your workplace?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, I do. And it’s not even like I’m sitting there trying to process the event or think about it on purpose. It’s more like... well, in my line of work, we talk about system interrupts, right? Like a hardware interrupt that just halts the processor and forces it to deal with a high-priority event immediately. That’s exactly what it feels like. It’s entirely involuntary. \n\nIt’s usually triggered by these really specific, otherwise completely mundane sensory inputs. Like... a badge scanner beeping. We have these RFID readers at the office, and they make this very distinct, sharp little chirp when someone badges in. Or, um, heavy footsteps in the hallway. You know how acoustics change when an office is mostly empty? The sound just carries differently, it echoes more. Or even something digital, like a late-night deployment alert pinging on my phone for a server update. \n\nWhen one of those things happens, it doesn't matter where I am or what I'm focusing on. It’s a total system override. I am suddenly put right back in that office on that night. It’s so incredibly visceral. My chest gets really tight, like I physically cannot draw a full breath, and... and then there's this image. The image of the security guard on the floor. It just violently cuts through whatever I’m doing. \n\nI could be mid-sentence in a team stand-up meeting, or deep into writing a complex block of code, or just standing in my kitchen making coffee, and suddenly I’m not there anymore. I’m right back in that moment, seeing him on the floor, hearing the sounds. And it takes me... I don't know, it takes a really long time to kind of reboot and convince my brain that I'm actually safe, that the event isn't happening right this second. It's just so exhausting because I can't control when someone is going to walk heavily down a hall or when a scanner is going to beep, so I'm just constantly vulnerable to my brain forcing me to relive the worst night of my life over and over again.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're observing yourself from outside your body or like you're in a dream when you're reliving the break-in?\n\nPatient: No, I... I don't think so. I mean, I know what you're talking about, I think. I read an article once about depersonalization, how the brain sort of detaches as a defense mechanism during trauma. Like you're watching a movie of yourself. But no, that's not what happens to me at all. It's actually the exact opposite. \n\nWhen I get pulled back into that memory, it's not like I'm floating above it or watching it happen to someone else. I am completely, horribly present in my own body. It's so visceral. Every physical sensation is magnified. The tightness in my chest, the way my heart starts hammering against my ribs, the sweat on my palms... it's all incredibly immediate. I'm not disconnected from it; I'm trapped in it. \n\nI almost wish I could disconnect, honestly. If I could just float away and watch it like a movie, maybe it wouldn't be so exhausting. But instead, it's like my nervous system is firing on all cylinders, screaming that the threat is right in front of me, right now. It's so grounding, but in the worst possible way. It anchors me to that panic. So, no, I don't feel like I'm in a dream or outside myself. I feel entirely too real, and entirely too stuck in that exact physical moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to avoid thinking or talking about the break-in at your workplace?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I don't know if \"try\" is the right word, because honestly, I don't really have to try. I just... don't. I mean, I don't go out of my way to bring it up, obviously. If you're asking if I actively suppress thoughts about it, it's hard to say. I guess I just try to keep my mind occupied with other things. \n\nLike, at work, I'll dive into a really complex data migration plan, or I'll spend hours debugging a script that probably doesn't even need that much attention, just to keep my processing power maxed out, you know? If my brain is running at 100% capacity on a logic problem, there's no bandwidth left for... for those memories to load. \n\nAnd as for talking about it, well, who am I going to talk to? My coworkers? They were either there and don't want to talk about it either, or they weren't and they just look at me with this... pity. I hate that look. My friends try, but they don't get it. They say things like, \"At least you weren't hurt,\" which, logically, yes, is true, but it completely misses the point of how it fundamentally altered my baseline operating state. So, I just don't bring it up. It's not like I have a sticky note on my monitor that says \"Don't think about the break-in,\" but I definitely don't invite the topic into my daily stand-ups or my personal life. It's just... easier to keep that file closed, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant problems in your personal relationships or daily life outside of work due to the way you've been feeling?\n\nPatient: Well, yes. Yes, I have. It’s... it’s actually been bleeding into everything, which is incredibly frustrating because I usually pride myself on compartmentalizing. But lately, it’s like my whole system architecture is just degrading. \n\nAt work, my performance has definitely slipped, and that’s hard for me to even admit out loud. I’ve always been the one people rely on to hit the mark, but recently, I’ve actually missed a couple of major data migration deadlines. I just... I get so caught up in trying to manage my own baseline anxiety, trying to keep those intrusive memories at bay, that my focus just shatters. And I’ve started actively avoiding key late-night deployments, which is a huge part of a systems analyst role. But being online late, seeing those alerts ping... it’s just too much of a trigger right now, so I make excuses or try to hand them off. \n\nIt’s gotten to the point where my manager actually brought it up in my last performance review. Not just the missed deadlines, but my attitude. Apparently, I’ve been getting into conflicts with my team, snapping at people over code reviews or system architecture disagreements. And that never used to happen. I was always the mediator, the calm one. But now, my fuse is just so short. I feel like I'm constantly running on empty, so any little friction just sparks a massive reaction. \n\nAnd outside of my direct job duties, it’s affecting the things I actually used to care about. I’ve completely pulled back from mentoring the junior analysts. I used to love doing that, helping them navigate the tech stack and the corporate environment. I was also really involved in these women-in-tech leadership events in the city—organizing panels, networking, speaking. But I haven't gone to a single one in months. I just... I don't feel steady anymore. I don't feel reliable. How can I stand up there and talk about leadership and resilience when I can barely get through a Tuesday without feeling like I'm going to jump out of my skin? So, I just isolate. I stay home. It’s safer, but it’s really lonely, and it feels like I’m losing all these parts of my identity that I worked so hard to build.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Sleep... yeah. Yeah, sleep is a whole other issue right now. It's like my brain just refuses to transition into rest mode, no matter how exhausted I am physically. I’ll do all the right things, you know? I’ll turn off all my screens an hour before bed, I’ll read a physical book, I’ll even try those guided meditation apps that everyone swears by, but it just doesn't matter. 

Most nights, I just lie there in the dark. And instead of drifting off, my mind starts spinning up all these background processes. I’ll start replaying little details from work, like a comment someone made during a sprint planning meeting, or I’ll obsess over a line of code that wasn’t compiling right. But it always, always circles back to the break-in. 

And then, when I finally do manage to fall asleep, it’s not even restful. I have these dreams... they’re so vivid. I’ll jolt awake, completely covered in sweat, because in the dream, I’m right back there. I’m hiding under that heavy oak conference table in the boardroom, trying to make myself as small as possible, listening to the sounds of them tearing the office apart outside the door. I can feel the cold floor against my knees, I can smell the stale coffee from the morning meeting... it’s terrifying. I wake up with my heart racing, and then, of course, I can’t get back to sleep for hours. 

Even on the rare nights where I manage to string together, say, six or seven hours of uninterrupted sleep, it doesn't feel like I actually slept. I wake up the next morning feeling completely drained. It’s like my system never actually powered down, you know? Like it was just running in a low-power state, constantly scanning for threats all night long. I drag myself out of bed feeling just as exhausted, if not more so, than when I lay down. It’s really taking a toll, because I just never feel recovered. I never feel rested.

I don't feel steady anymore. I don't feel reliable. How can I stand up there and talk about leadership and resilience when I can barely get through a Tuesday without feeling like I'm going to jump out of my skin? So, I just isolate. I stay home. It’s safer, but it’s really lonely, and it feels like I’m losing all these parts of my identity that I worked so hard to build.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping at night?\n\nPatient: Sleep... yeah. Yeah, sleep is a whole other issue right now. It's like my brain just refuses to transition into rest mode, no matter how exhausted I am physically. I’ll do all the right things, you know? I’ll turn off all my screens an hour before bed, I’ll read a physical book, I’ll even try those guided meditation apps that everyone swears by, but it just doesn't matter. \n\nMost nights, I just lie there in the dark. I’ll start replaying little details from work, like a comment someone made during a sprint planning meeting, or I’ll obsess over a line of code that wasn’t compiling right. I wake up with my heart racing, and then, of course, I can’t get back to sleep for hours. \n\nEven on the rare nights where I manage to string together, say, six or seven hours of uninterrupted sleep, it doesn't feel like I actually slept. I wake up the next morning feeling completely drained. It’s like my system never actually powered down, you know? Like it was just running in a low-power state, constantly scanning for threats all night long. I drag myself out of bed feeling just as exhausted, if not more so, than when I lay down. Concentrate? Um... actually, no, I wouldn't say I have trouble concentrating. I mean, I know I just talked about how my focus shatters when I'm dealing with the anxiety and the memories, but that's... that's different. It's just that the environment around the task has become so fraught. The actual cognitive ability to focus on the work in front of me, when I'm not actively fighting off a panic response, feels completely intact. It's just the noise around it that's the problem, if that makes sense. So, yeah, the concentration itself isn't broken. It's just... heavily besieged sometimes. But I can still focus when I need to.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Guilt... yeah. Yeah, I do. It’s actually something that’s been eating away at me pretty consistently, which is awful because, logically, I know how irrational it sounds. But the logic just doesn't seem to matter when I'm lying awake at night or when my mind starts drifting during the day. 

I just keep replaying the whole scenario, specifically regarding the overnight deployment. We were pushing a massive system update, and I was the one managing the timeline. I knew we’d be there late. I knew the building would be mostly empty. And I just keep thinking... I keep thinking that if I had just insisted on better security protocols for that night, or if I had maybe escalated my concerns about the lack of after-hours personnel to the building management beforehand, things would have been different. I mean, we're dealing with sensitive data, we should have had a tighter perimeter anyway. 

But beyond the prep, it's the actual event. When they broke in... I was in the boardroom, like I said, hiding under the table. And I heard them coming down the hall. I heard the commotion. And I had my phone in my hand, but I was just so frozen. I was so terrified that the screen lighting up or the sound of me whispering to a 911 operator would draw them right to me. So I waited. I waited what felt like an eternity before I finally made the call. 

And the security guard... he was the one who intercepted them first. He got hurt. He got hurt really badly. And I just... I can't shake this thought, this heavy, suffocating thought, that if I had just called sooner, if I had just overcome my own panic for two seconds to dial the police the absolute second I heard the glass shatter, maybe they would have gotten there in time. Maybe the guard wouldn't have been injured. 

Part of me—the rational, analytical part of my brain—knows that I was just a civilian in a terrifying situation, that I was hiding for my life, and that I couldn't have predicted what those guys were going to do. But the rest of me just feels entirely responsible. Like my inaction, my delay, directly caused his injuries. It’s like I failed a critical system check, and someone else paid the price for it. So, yes, the guilt is... it's overwhelming sometimes. I just can't stop blaming myself for not doing more, or doing it faster.

But it always, always circles back to the break-in. \n\nAnd then, when I finally do manage to fall asleep, it’s not even restful. I have these dreams... they’re so vivid. I’ll jolt awake, completely covered in sweat, because in the dream, I’m right back there. I’m hiding under that heavy oak conference table in the boardroom, trying to make myself as small as possible, listening to the sounds of them tearing the office apart outside the door. I can feel the cold floor against my knees, I can smell the stale coffee from the morning meeting... it’s terrifying. I wake up with my heart racing, and then, of course, I can’t get back to sleep for hours. \n\nEven on the rare nights where I manage to string together, say, six or seven hours of uninterrupted sleep, it doesn't feel like I actually slept. I wake up the next morning feeling completely drained. It’s like my system never actually powered down, you know? Like it was just running in a low-power state, constantly scanning for threats all night long. I drag myself out of bed feeling just as exhausted, if not more so, than when I lay down. It’s really taking a toll, because I just never feel recovered. I never feel rested.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to concentrate on things you're trying to do?\n\nPatient: Concentrate? Um... actually, no, I wouldn't say I have trouble concentrating. I mean, I know I just talked about how my focus shatters when I'm dealing with the anxiety and the memories, but that's... that's different. That's me being overwhelmed by a specific stimulus, you know? It's like a system overload, not a hardware failure. \n\nWhen I actually sit down to do a task, if I can get into the zone, my concentration is... well, it's fine. It's the same as it always was. Like, if I'm building a new SQL query or mapping out a network topology, I can still lock in. I can still trace the logic paths and hold all the variables in my head. I don't find myself reading the same paragraph over and over, or forgetting what I was doing in the middle of a task, unless, of course, a memory spikes. \n\nBut just general, day-to-day concentration? No, I don't think that's an issue. I can still follow complex conversations, I can still analyze data sets. It's just that the environment around the task has become so fraught. The actual cognitive ability to focus on the work in front of me, when I'm not actively fighting off a panic response, feels completely intact. It's just the noise around it that's the problem, if that makes sense. So, yeah, the concentration itself isn't broken. It's just... heavily besieged sometimes. But I can still focus when I need to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything related to the break-in at your workplace?\n\nPatient: Guilt... yeah. Yeah, I do. It’s actually something that’s been eating away at me pretty consistently, which is awful because, logically, I know how irrational it sounds. But the logic just doesn't seem to matter when I'm lying awake at night or when my mind starts drifting during the day. \n\nI just keep replaying the whole scenario, specifically regarding the overnight deployment. We were pushing a massive system update, and I was the one managing the timeline. I knew we’d be there late. I knew the building would be mostly empty. And I just keep thinking... I keep thinking that if I had just insisted on better security protocols for that night, or if I had maybe escalated my concerns about the lack of after-hours personnel to the building management beforehand, things would have been different. I mean, we're dealing with sensitive data, we should have had a tighter perimeter anyway. \n\nBut beyond the prep, it's the actual event. When they broke in... I was in the boardroom, like I said, hiding under the table. And I heard them coming down the hall. I heard the commotion. And I had my phone in my hand, but I was just so frozen. I was so terrified that the screen lighting up or the sound of me whispering to a 911 operator would draw them right to me. So I waited. I waited what felt like an eternity before I finally made the call. \n\nAnd the security guard... he was the one who intercepted them first. He got hurt. He got hurt really badly. And I just... I can't shake this thought, this heavy, suffocating thought, that if I had just called sooner, if I had just overcome my own panic for two seconds to dial the police the absolute second I heard the glass shatter, maybe they would have gotten there in time. Maybe the guard wouldn't have been injured. \n\nPart of me—the rational, analytical part of my brain—knows that I was just a civilian in a terrifying situation, that I was hiding for my life, and that I couldn't have predicted what those guys were going to do. But the rest of me just feels entirely responsible. Like my inaction, my delay, directly caused his injuries. It’s like I failed a critical system check, and someone else paid the price for it. So, yes, the guilt is... it's overwhelming sometimes. Interest in things... you mean like hobbies and stuff? Um, no, I don't think so. I mean, I know sometimes people get depressed after something like this and just stop caring about everything, but that hasn't really happened to me. I still like the things I used to like. 

Like, I'm still really into building my custom mechanical keyboards. I actually just finished a new build last weekend—a 65% layout with a brass plate and these really nice, tactile Holy Panda switches that I had to lube by hand, which takes forever, but the sound profile is just so satisfying when you get it right. I can still spend hours researching different keycap sets or watching typing test videos on YouTube. It's actually one of the few things that kind of forces me to focus on something physical, something right in front of me, instead of getting lost in my head. 

And I still really enjoy hiking, too. I haven't been out as much lately, mostly just because I've been so exhausted from the lack of sleep we talked about, but the desire to go is definitely still there. I was actually just looking at trail maps for this state park a couple of hours north of here, trying to plan a weekend trip for when the leaves start changing. I love that crisp autumn air and just getting away from all the screens and the noise of the city. 

So, yeah, the actual interest in my hobbies is totally still intact. It's not like the color has drained out of the world or anything. I still want to do things, I still find joy in the things I've always loved. It's just that sometimes the logistics of actually doing them—like finding the energy when I'm running on three hours of terrible sleep, or dealing with the anxiety of going to a crowded trailhead—can be a barrier. But the core interest, the actual enjoyment of the activity itself once I'm doing it? That hasn't gone anywhere. I still very much care about my interests.

Not just the missed deadlines, but my attitude. Apparently, I’ve been getting into conflicts with my team, snapping at people over code reviews or system architecture disagreements. And that never used to happen. I was always the mediator, the calm one. But now, my fuse is just so short. I feel like I'm constantly running on empty, so any little friction just sparks a massive reaction. \n\nAnd outside of my direct job duties, it’s affecting the things I actually used to care about. I’ve completely pulled back from mentoring the junior analysts. I used to love doing that, helping them navigate the tech stack and the corporate environment. I was also really involved in these women-in-tech leadership events in the city—organizing panels, networking, speaking. But I haven't gone to a single one in months. I just... I don't feel steady anymore. I don't feel reliable. How can I stand up there and talk about leadership and resilience when I can barely get through a Tuesday without feeling like I'm going to jump out of my skin? So, I just isolate. I stay home. Yeah, sleep is a whole other issue right now. It's like my brain just refuses to transition into rest mode, no matter how exhausted I am physically. I’ll do all the right things, you know? I’ll turn off all my screens an hour before bed, I’ll read a physical book, I’ll even try those guided meditation apps that everyone swears by, but it just doesn't matter. \n\nMost nights, I just lie there in the dark. I wake up the next morning feeling completely drained. It’s like my system never actually powered down, you know? Maybe the guard wouldn't have been injured. \n\nPart of me—the rational, analytical part of my brain—knows that I was just a civilian in a terrifying situation, that I was hiding for my life, and that I couldn't have predicted what those guys were going to do. But the rest of me just feels entirely responsible. Like my inaction, my delay, directly caused his injuries. It’s like I failed a critical system check, and someone else paid the price for it. I just can't stop blaming myself for not doing more, or doing it faster.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: Interest in things... you mean like hobbies and stuff? I can still spend hours researching different keycap sets or watching typing test videos on YouTube. It's actually one of the few things that kind of forces me to focus on something physical, something right in front of me, instead of getting lost in my head. \n\nAnd I still really enjoy hiking, too. I love that crisp autumn air and just getting away from all the screens and the noise of the city. \n\nSo, yeah, the actual interest in my hobbies is totally still intact. Physical health? Um... no, not really. I mean, I know I've talked a lot about the sleep issues and the anxiety, and obviously, those take a physical toll—like, I feel tired, and sometimes my muscles are tense from just being on edge all the time. But as far as actual, distinct changes in my physical health? No, I don't think there's anything new or concerning on that front. 

I haven't had any weird aches or pains that I can't explain, no changes in my appetite or digestion, nothing like that. I still try to eat relatively healthy, you know, meal prepping on Sundays so I don't just end up ordering takeout every night when I'm too exhausted to cook. I usually make a big batch of quinoa and roasted vegetables, maybe some grilled chicken. It's just easier that way, especially with my schedule. And I still try to stay somewhat active, even if I haven't been hiking as much as I'd like. I have a standing desk at home, and I try to make sure I'm not just sitting in one position for eight hours straight while I'm working. 

I actually had my annual physical with my primary care doctor a couple of weeks ago—well, maybe it was three weeks ago now, time has been a little blurry lately—but anyway, everything checked out fine. My blood pressure was normal, my heart rate was fine, all my blood work came back completely standard. No vitamin deficiencies or anything like that, which I was a little worried about since I haven't been getting out in the sun as much. But nope, all good. 

So, yeah, physically, I seem to be holding up okay. It's really just the mental and emotional stuff that's been thrown completely out of whack since... well, since everything happened. My body itself seems to be functioning just fine, all things considered. I haven't noticed any weird rashes, no hair loss, no sudden weight changes, nothing that would make me think my physical health has deteriorated. It's just... it's just my brain that feels like it's constantly misfiring. But physically? No, I'm okay.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Um, thank you. I’m here because... well, it’s a little hard to explain, but I just really need some help figuring out what's going on in my head. I work as a computer systems analyst, and I actually finished my graduate degree not too long ago, so I’ve always been someone who relies on logic and fixing things. I used to be so on top of everything. But lately, I just... I can’t shake the feeling that work isn’t safe anymore. \n\nAnd it’s not just about the building or the office, it’s this heavy, constant weight that I’m somehow fundamentally damaged now. I sit at my desk, looking at my monitors and the projects I'm supposed to be managing, and I just feel like the competent, driven person who worked so hard and got promoted just... isn’t really there anymore. It’s like she’s completely gone, and I’m just this broken version trying to pretend I still know how to function. I keep trying to analyze my way out of it—because that’s what I do, I find the flaw in the system and I patch it—but no matter how much I try to reason with myself, I just have this overwhelming belief that I'm ruined and that nothing around me is secure. I don't know if that makes any sense to you, but I just keep spinning my wheels on this, and it's exhausting trying to hide the fact that I feel so entirely different inside.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Well, I know exactly when it started, actually. It’s been... let’s see, about two years and four months now. I can pinpoint the exact timeline because it all traces back to this one specific project we were doing. In my line of work, when we have to push major system updates or do server migrations, we have to do overnight deployments. You know, you have to do it when all the users are offline so you don't disrupt the normal business hours or crash the network while people are trying to work. \n\nSo, I was there late, doing this overnight deployment, just running my scripts and watching the progress bars on my monitors. It was supposed to be quiet, just me and the code, but... that's when the break-in happened. The building was broken into while I was there working. And ever since that specific night, two years and four months ago, this whole feeling just completely latched onto me. I keep looking at the calendar and calculating the time—like, twenty-eight months should be enough time to process an event logically, right? You'd think after more than two years you could just isolate the error, patch the bug, and move on. But ever since that break-in during the deployment, I just... I haven't been able to reset back to the person I was before that night. It's just been this continuous, unbroken loop since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes where you felt an intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes, with symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or shortness of breath?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I know what you're talking about, like a panic attack, right? I've read about those because, obviously, when my brain started glitching after the break-in, I started researching everything. I looked up anxiety, stress responses, the sympathetic nervous system, all of it. I wanted to understand the mechanics of what was happening to me. So I know the checklist—the hyperventilating, the feeling like you're having a heart attack, the sudden peak of terror out of nowhere. But I don't really get those. \n\nIt's not like a sudden spike for me. It's more like... a constant hum in the background. Like a server that's running too hot, all the time. My heart doesn't suddenly start pounding out of my chest, and I don't start gasping for air or sweating through my clothes in the middle of a meeting. It's just this heavy, persistent dread. It's like I'm always waiting for the system to crash, rather than actually experiencing the crash itself, if that makes sense. I don't get those sudden, intense episodes where I feel like I'm dying or losing control in a matter of minutes. It's just a steady state of feeling unsafe.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this sense of being damaged and unsafe only at work, or does it also affect how you feel in other parts of your life, like with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Well, it definitely bleeds over, but it’s most obvious at work because that’s where the metrics are, you know? Like, I can track the decline. It’s not just a feeling; it’s actually impacting my deliverables. My work has slipped enough that I’ve actually missed migration deadlines. And I never used to miss deadlines. I was the person who built the project timelines, so missing them is just... it’s completely unacceptable to me. But I’ve been actively avoiding key late deployments. If a script needs to be run at 2:00 AM, I’ll find an excuse to hand it off, or I’ll try to automate it in a way that’s riskier just so I don’t have to be the one monitoring it late at night. \n\nAnd my boss noticed. I actually got called out in my last performance review, which was devastating. It wasn't just the deadlines, either; they mentioned that I’ve been having conflicts with the team, getting irritable or snapping at people over code reviews or minor server configurations. And that never used to happen. I used to be the one who smoothed things over between the developers and the infrastructure guys. \n\nBut it’s not just the technical work, either. I used to be really involved in our mentorship program, and I was on the organizing committee for these women-in-tech leadership events in the city. I loved doing that, connecting with younger women entering the field, helping them navigate the corporate structure. But I’ve completely pulled back from all of it. I stepped down from the committee and I haven't taken on a new mentee in over a year. I just... I don’t feel steady anymore. I don't feel reliable. How can I sit there and give someone career advice or talk about leadership when I feel like my own internal architecture is collapsing? I just feel like a fraud trying to project this image of a successful, put-together analyst when, underneath it all, I'm dropping the ball on the most basic parts of my job.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid places or situations that remind you of the break-in at your workplace?\n\nPatient: Well, yes. Yes, I do. And it’s actually become this whole... logistical nightmare, to be honest. I mean, my job is literally about optimizing systems and creating efficient workflows, but my own daily routine has become this incredibly convoluted, inefficient process just to avoid anything that triggers that feeling. \n\nFor instance, the building itself. I used to just walk through the main lobby, take the elevator, normal stuff. But now, if I have to be there anywhere near the evening, I absolutely cannot use the main entrance. I’ve started taking this side entrance that goes through the old loading dock area, which is actually less secure logically, but for some reason, walking through that main lobby after dark just... it makes me feel like I’m going to come apart. Like my chest gets tight and I just start visualizing the glass breaking again. \n\nSo I avoid the lobby. But it’s more than just the physical space, it’s the timing of the work itself. Like I mentioned before about the overnight deployments—I’ve been actively trading shifts with the other analysts. I’ll take their early morning database maintenance windows, which nobody wants because you have to log on at 4:30 AM, just so they’ll take my late-night release windows. And if I can’t trade, I’ve started pushing back on the release windows entirely. I’ll argue in the sprint planning meetings that we need to schedule the deployment for a weekend afternoon instead of a weeknight, citing user impact metrics that are, frankly, a bit of a stretch, just to justify not being in that building or even online late at night. \n\nAnd honestly, I’ve just been working from home as much as humanly possible. Our company has a hybrid policy, three days in, two days remote, but I’ve been stretching that to the absolute limit. I’ll claim I have a contractor coming, or I’m feeling slightly under the weather, or I need \"deep focus time\" for a complex script—anything to avoid going into the office, especially if I know I might have to stay past 5:00 PM. Because the second the sun starts going down and the office starts emptying out, that hum I told you about? It gets so loud I can't even look at my monitors. So yeah, I avoid it. I avoid the lobby, I avoid the late shifts, I avoid the building itself whenever I can. It's like I'm constantly rewriting my own schedule just to route around this massive error in my system.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself remembering or reliving the break-in at your workplace?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, I do. And it’s not even like I’m sitting there trying to process the event or think about it on purpose. It’s more like... well, in my line of work, we talk about system interrupts, right? Like a hardware interrupt that just halts the processor and forces it to deal with a high-priority event immediately. That’s exactly what it feels like. It’s entirely involuntary. \n\nIt’s usually triggered by these really specific, otherwise completely mundane sensory inputs. Like... a badge scanner beeping. We have these RFID readers at the office, and they make this very distinct, sharp little chirp when someone badges in. Or, um, heavy footsteps in the hallway. You know how acoustics change when an office is mostly empty? The sound just carries differently, it echoes more. Or even something digital, like a late-night deployment alert pinging on my phone for a server update. \n\nWhen one of those things happens, it doesn't matter where I am or what I'm focusing on. It’s a total system override. I am suddenly put right back in that office on that night. It’s so incredibly visceral. My chest gets really tight, like I physically cannot draw a full breath, and... and then there's this image. The image of the security guard on the floor. It just violently cuts through whatever I’m doing. \n\nI could be mid-sentence in a team stand-up meeting, or deep into writing a complex block of code, or just standing in my kitchen making coffee, and suddenly I’m not there anymore. I’m right back in that moment, seeing him on the floor, hearing the sounds. And it takes me... I don't know, it takes a really long time to kind of reboot and convince my brain that I'm actually safe, that the event isn't happening right this second. It's just so exhausting because I can't control when someone is going to walk heavily down a hall or when a scanner is going to beep, so I'm just constantly vulnerable to my brain forcing me to relive the worst night of my life over and over again.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're observing yourself from outside your body or like you're in a dream when you're reliving the break-in?\n\nPatient: No, I... I don't think so. I mean, I know what you're talking about, I think. I read an article once about depersonalization, how the brain sort of detaches as a defense mechanism during trauma. Like you're watching a movie of yourself. But no, that's not what happens to me at all. It's actually the exact opposite. \n\nWhen I get pulled back into that memory, it's not like I'm floating above it or watching it happen to someone else. I am completely, horribly present in my own body. It's so visceral. Every physical sensation is magnified. The tightness in my chest, the way my heart starts hammering against my ribs, the sweat on my palms... it's all incredibly immediate. I'm not disconnected from it; I'm trapped in it. \n\nI almost wish I could disconnect, honestly. If I could just float away and watch it like a movie, maybe it wouldn't be so exhausting. But instead, it's like my nervous system is firing on all cylinders, screaming that the threat is right in front of me, right now. It's so grounding, but in the worst possible way. It anchors me to that panic. So, no, I don't feel like I'm in a dream or outside myself. I feel entirely too real, and entirely too stuck in that exact physical moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to avoid thinking or talking about the break-in at your workplace?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I don't know if \"try\" is the right word, because honestly, I don't really have to try. I just... don't. I mean, I don't go out of my way to bring it up, obviously. If you're asking if I actively suppress thoughts about it, it's hard to say. I guess I just try to keep my mind occupied with other things. \n\nLike, at work, I'll dive into a really complex data migration plan, or I'll spend hours debugging a script that probably doesn't even need that much attention, just to keep my processing power maxed out, you know? If my brain is running at 100% capacity on a logic problem, there's no bandwidth left for... for those memories to load. \n\nAnd as for talking about it, well, who am I going to talk to? My coworkers? They were either there and don't want to talk about it either, or they weren't and they just look at me with this... pity. I hate that look. My friends try, but they don't get it. They say things like, \"At least you weren't hurt,\" which, logically, yes, is true, but it completely misses the point of how it fundamentally altered my baseline operating state. So, I just don't bring it up. It's not like I have a sticky note on my monitor that says \"Don't think about the break-in,\" but I definitely don't invite the topic into my daily stand-ups or my personal life. It's just... easier to keep that file closed, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant problems in your personal relationships or daily life outside of work due to the way you've been feeling?\n\nPatient: Well, yes. Yes, I have. It’s... it’s actually been bleeding into everything, which is incredibly frustrating because I usually pride myself on compartmentalizing. But lately, it’s like my whole system architecture is just degrading. \n\nAt work, my performance has definitely slipped, and that’s hard for me to even admit out loud. I’ve always been the one people rely on to hit the mark, but recently, I’ve actually missed a couple of major data migration deadlines. I just... I get so caught up in trying to manage my own baseline anxiety, trying to keep those intrusive memories at bay, that my focus just shatters. And I’ve started actively avoiding key late-night deployments, which is a huge part of a systems analyst role. And that never used to happen. I was always the mediator, the calm one. But now, my fuse is just so short. I feel like I'm constantly running on empty, so any little friction just sparks a massive reaction. \n\nAnd outside of my direct job duties, it’s affecting the things I actually used to care about. I’ve completely pulled back from mentoring the junior analysts. I used to love doing that, helping them navigate the tech stack and the corporate environment. I was also really involved in these women-in-tech leadership events in the city—organizing panels, networking, speaking. But I haven't gone to a single one in months. I just... I don't feel steady anymore. I don't feel reliable. How can I stand up there and talk about leadership and resilience when I can barely get through a Tuesday without feeling like I'm going to jump out of my skin? So, I just isolate. I stay home. It’s safer, but it’s really lonely, and it feels like I’m losing all these parts of my identity that I worked so hard to build.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping at night?\n\nPatient: Sleep... yeah. Yeah, sleep is a whole other issue right now. It's like my brain just refuses to transition into rest mode, no matter how exhausted I am physically. I’ll do all the right things, you know? I’ll turn off all my screens an hour before bed, I’ll read a physical book, I’ll even try those guided meditation apps that everyone swears by, but it just doesn't matter. \n\nMost nights, I just lie there in the dark. And instead of drifting off, my mind starts spinning up all these background processes. I’ll start replaying little details from work, like a comment someone made during a sprint planning meeting, or I’ll obsess over a line of code that wasn’t compiling right. But it always, always circles back to the break-in. \n\nAnd then, when I finally do manage to fall asleep, it’s not even restful. I have these dreams... they’re so vivid. I’ll jolt awake, completely covered in sweat, because in the dream, I’m right back there. I’m hiding under that heavy oak conference table in the boardroom, trying to make myself as small as possible, listening to the sounds of them tearing the office apart outside the door. I can feel the cold floor against my knees, I can smell the stale coffee from the morning meeting... it’s terrifying. I wake up with my heart racing, and then, of course, I can’t get back to sleep for hours. \n\nEven on the rare nights where I manage to string together, say, six or seven hours of uninterrupted sleep, it doesn't feel like I actually slept. I wake up the next morning feeling completely drained. It’s like my system never actually powered down, you know? Like it was just running in a low-power state, constantly scanning for threats all night long. I drag myself out of bed feeling just as exhausted, if not more so, than when I lay down. If you're asking if I actively suppress thoughts about it, it's hard to say. I guess I just try to keep my mind occupied with other things. \n\nLike, at work, I'll dive into a really complex data migration plan, or I'll spend hours debugging a script that probably doesn't even need that much attention, just to keep my processing power maxed out, you know? If my brain is running at 100% capacity on a logic problem, there's no bandwidth left for... for those memories to load. \n\nAnd as for talking about it, well, who am I going to talk to? My coworkers? They were either there and don't want to talk about it either, or they weren't and they just look at me with this... pity. I hate that look. My friends try, but they don't get it. They say things like, \"At least you weren't hurt,\" which, logically, yes, is true, but it completely misses the point of how it fundamentally altered my baseline operating state. So, I just don't bring it up. It's not like I have a sticky note on my monitor that says \"Don't think about the break-in,\" but I definitely don't invite the topic into my daily stand-ups or my personal life. It's just... easier to keep that file closed, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant problems in your personal relationships or daily life outside of work due to the way you've been feeling?\n\nPatient: Well, yes. Yes, I have. It’s... it’s actually been bleeding into everything, which is incredibly frustrating because I usually pride myself on compartmentalizing. But lately, it’s like my whole system architecture is just degrading. \n\nAt work, my performance has definitely slipped, and that’s hard for me to even admit out loud. I’ve always been the one people rely on to hit the mark, but recently, I’ve actually missed a couple of major data migration deadlines. I just... I get so caught up in trying to manage my own baseline anxiety, trying to keep those intrusive memories at bay, that my focus just shatters. And I’ve started actively avoiding key late-night deployments, which is a huge part of a systems analyst role. But being online late, seeing those alerts ping... it’s just too much of a trigger right now, so I make excuses or try to hand them off. \n\nIt’s gotten to the point where my manager actually brought it up in my last performance review. Not just the missed deadlines, but my attitude. Apparently, I’ve been getting into conflicts with my team, snapping at people over code reviews or system architecture disagreements. And that never used to happen. I was always the mediator, the calm one. But now, my fuse is just so short. I feel like I'm constantly running on empty, so any little friction just sparks a massive reaction. \n\nAnd outside of my direct job duties, it’s affecting the things I actually used to care about. I’ve completely pulled back from mentoring the junior analysts. I used to love doing that, helping them navigate the tech stack and the corporate environment. I was also really involved in these women-in-tech leadership events in the city—organizing panels, networking, speaking. But I haven't gone to a single one in months. I just... I don't feel steady anymore. I don't feel reliable. How can I stand up there and talk about leadership and resilience when I can barely get through a Tuesday without feeling like I'm going to jump out of my skin? So, I just isolate. I stay home. It’s safer, but it’s really lonely, and it feels like I’m losing all these parts of my identity that I worked so hard to build.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping at night?\n\nPatient: Sleep... yeah. Yeah, sleep is a whole other issue right now. It's like my brain just refuses to transition into rest mode, no matter how exhausted I am physically. I’ll do all the right things, you know? I’ll turn off all my screens an hour before bed, I’ll read a physical book, I’ll even try those guided meditation apps that everyone swears by, but it just doesn't matter. \n\nMost nights, I just lie there in the dark. And instead of drifting off, my mind starts spinning up all these background processes. I’ll start replaying little details from work, like a comment someone made during a sprint planning meeting, or I’ll obsess over a line of code that wasn’t compiling right. But it always, always circles back to the break-in. \n\nAnd then, when I finally do manage to fall asleep, it’s not even restful. I have these dreams... they’re so vivid. I’ll jolt awake, completely covered in sweat, because in the dream, I’m right back there. I’m hiding under that heavy oak conference table in the boardroom, trying to make myself as small as possible, listening to the sounds of them tearing the office apart outside the door. I can feel the cold floor against my knees, I can smell the stale coffee from the morning meeting... it’s terrifying. I wake up with my heart racing, and then, of course, I can’t get back to sleep for hours. \n\nEven on the rare nights where I manage to string together, say, six or seven hours of uninterrupted sleep, it doesn't feel like I actually slept. I wake up the next morning feeling completely drained. It’s like my system never actually powered down, you know? Like it was just running in a low-power state, constantly scanning for threats all night long. I drag myself out of bed feeling just as exhausted, if not more so, than when I lay down. It’s really taking a toll, because I just never feel recovered. I never feel rested.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to concentrate on things you're trying to do?\n\nPatient: Concentrate? Um... actually, no, I wouldn't say I have trouble concentrating. I mean, I know I just talked about how my focus shatters when I'm dealing with the anxiety and the memories, but that's... that's different. That's me being overwhelmed by a specific stimulus, you know? It's like a system overload, not a hardware failure. \n\nWhen I actually sit down to do a task, if I can get into the zone, my concentration is... well, it's fine. It's the same as it always was. Like, if I'm building a new SQL query or mapping out a network topology, I can still lock in. I can still trace the logic paths and hold all the variables in my head. I don't find myself reading the same paragraph over and over, or forgetting what I was doing in the middle of a task, unless, of course, a memory spikes. \n\nBut just general, day-to-day concentration? No, I don't think that's an issue. I can still follow complex conversations, I can still analyze data sets. It's just that the environment around the task has become so fraught. The actual cognitive ability to focus on the work in front of me, when I'm not actively fighting off a panic response, feels completely intact. It's just the noise around it that's the problem, if that makes sense. So, yeah, the concentration itself isn't broken. It's just... heavily besieged sometimes. But I can still focus when I need to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything related to the break-in at your workplace?\n\nPatient: Guilt... yeah. Yeah, I do. It’s actually something that’s been eating away at me pretty consistently, which is awful because, logically, I know how irrational it sounds. But the logic just doesn't seem to matter when I'm lying awake at night or when my mind starts drifting during the day. \n\nI just keep replaying the whole scenario, specifically regarding the overnight deployment. We were pushing a massive system update, and I was the one managing the timeline. I knew we’d be there late. I knew the building would be mostly empty. And I just keep thinking... I keep thinking that if I had just insisted on better security protocols for that night, or if I had maybe escalated my concerns about the lack of after-hours personnel to the building management beforehand, things would have been different. I mean, we're dealing with sensitive data, we should have had a tighter perimeter anyway. \n\nBut beyond the prep, it's the actual event. When they broke in... I was in the boardroom, like I said, hiding under the table. And I heard them coming down the hall. I heard the commotion. And I had my phone in my hand, but I was just so frozen. I was so terrified that the screen lighting up or the sound of me whispering to a 911 operator would draw them right to me. So I waited. I waited what felt like an eternity before I finally made the call. \n\nAnd the security guard... he was the one who intercepted them first. He got hurt. He got hurt really badly. And I just... I can't shake this thought, this heavy, suffocating thought, that if I had just called sooner, if I had just overcome my own panic for two seconds to dial the police the absolute second I heard the glass shatter, maybe they would have gotten there in time. Maybe the guard wouldn't have been injured. \n\nPart of me—the rational, analytical part of my brain—knows that I was just a civilian in a terrifying situation, that I was hiding for my life, and that I couldn't have predicted what those guys were going to do. But the rest of me just feels entirely responsible. Like my inaction, my delay, directly caused his injuries. It’s like I failed a critical system check, and someone else paid the price for it. So, yes, the guilt is... it's overwhelming sometimes. I just can't stop blaming myself for not doing more, or doing it faster.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: Interest in things... you mean like hobbies and stuff? Um, no, I don't think so. I mean, I know sometimes people get depressed after something like this and just stop caring about everything, but that hasn't really happened to me. I still like the things I used to like. \n\nLike, I'm still really into building my custom mechanical keyboards. I actually just finished a new build last weekend—a 65% layout with a brass plate and these really nice, tactile Holy Panda switches that I had to lube by hand, which takes forever, but the sound profile is just so satisfying when you get it right. I can still spend hours researching different keycap sets or watching typing test videos on YouTube. It's actually one of the few things that kind of forces me to focus on something physical, something right in front of me, instead of getting lost in my head. \n\nAnd I still really enjoy hiking, too. I haven't been out as much lately, mostly just because I've been so exhausted from the lack of sleep we talked about, but the desire to go is definitely still there. I was actually just looking at trail maps for this state park a couple of hours north of here, trying to plan a weekend trip for when the leaves start changing. I love that crisp autumn air and just getting away from all the screens and the noise of the city. \n\nSo, yeah, the actual interest in my hobbies is totally still intact. It's not like the color has drained out of the world or anything. I still want to do things, I still find joy in the things I've always loved. It's just that sometimes the logistics of actually doing them—like finding the energy when I'm running on three hours of terrible sleep, or dealing with the anxiety of going to a crowded trailhead—can be a barrier. But the core interest, the actual enjoyment of the activity itself once I'm doing it? That hasn't gone anywhere. I still very much care about my interests.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your physical health since the break-in?\n\nPatient: Physical health? Um... no, not really. I mean, I know I've talked a lot about the sleep issues and the anxiety, and obviously, those take a physical toll—like, I feel tired, and sometimes my muscles are tense from just being on edge all the time. But as far as actual, distinct changes in my physical health? No, I don't think there's anything new or concerning on that front. \n\nI haven't had any weird aches or pains that I can't explain, no changes in my appetite or digestion, nothing like that. I still try to eat relatively healthy, you know, meal prepping on Sundays so I don't just end up ordering takeout every night when I'm too exhausted to cook. I usually make a big batch of quinoa and roasted vegetables, maybe some grilled chicken. It's just easier that way, especially with my schedule. And I still try to stay somewhat active, even if I haven't been hiking as much as I'd like. I have a standing desk at home, and I try to make sure I'm not just sitting in one position for eight hours straight while I'm working. \n\nI actually had my annual physical with my primary care doctor a couple of weeks ago—well, maybe it was three weeks ago now, time has been a little blurry lately—but anyway, everything checked out fine. My blood pressure was normal, my heart rate was fine, all my blood work came back completely standard. No vitamin deficiencies or anything like that, which I was a little worried about since I haven't been getting out in the sun as much. But nope, all good. \n\nSo, yeah, physically, I seem to be holding up okay. It's really just the mental and emotional stuff that's been thrown completely out of whack since... well, since everything happened. My body itself seems to be functioning just fine, all things considered. I haven't noticed any weird rashes, no hair loss, no sudden weight changes, nothing that would make me think my physical health has deteriorated. It's just... it's just my brain that feels like it's constantly misfiring. But physically? No, I'm okay.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Irritable? Yeah... yeah, I definitely do. It's actually been causing some real friction at work lately, which is so frustrating because I've always prided myself on being really level-headed professionally, you know? I'm an analyst, I'm supposed to be objective. But lately, my fuse is just... it's practically non-existent. 

It happens a lot during our sprint meetings or when we're evaluating peer work. Like, in code reviews I snap at people over small things, like a sloppy rollback plan or someone questioning my timeline, and then I hear myself sounding harsh and personal before I can pull it back. It’s like this immediate, uncontrollable spike of rage. A rollback plan is crucial, obviously, but instead of just pointing out the gaps constructively like I used to, I just go on the offensive. 

And the worst part is that split second where I'm talking, and I can hear the tone of my own voice—I can hear how aggressive and mean I'm being to a coworker who just asked a simple question about a deadline—but the words are already spilling out. And then I have to sit there and try to backtrack or do damage control, apologize in Slack later, which is just exhausting and embarrassing. 

I never used to be like this. I used to be the one who could calmly walk a junior developer through their mistakes without making them feel stupid, or negotiate a deployment schedule without getting defensive. But now, it's like every little question or minor error feels like this massive, irritating obstacle, and I just bite their head off before my rational brain can even catch up. It's like I have absolutely no buffer between feeling mildly annoyed and just completely lashing out at people who really don't deserve it.

Detached? Like... like I'm not really here? Um, no, I don't think I've ever felt anything like that. I mean, I know what you're talking about, I've read about people feeling like they're watching themselves from the outside or like the world around them isn't real, like a movie or something. But no, I haven't experienced that. 

If anything, I feel almost too attached, you know? Like I'm hyper-aware of everything around me, every sound, every movement. It's the opposite of being detached. I'm constantly scanning my environment, constantly feeling every single thing, especially the anxiety and the anger I mentioned. It's all very, very real and immediate to me. 

I've never had a moment where I looked at my hands and felt like they weren't mine, or walked down the street and felt like I was in a dream. My surroundings are almost aggressively real to me right now. Like, when I'm in my apartment, I'm so aware of the door, the windows, the sounds in the hallway. I'm definitely not floating away from it all. I'm very much stuck right here in my own head and my own body, for better or worse. So, no, no feelings of detachment or unreality or anything like that. Just... just very grounded, unfortunately grounded, in everything that's going on.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Um, thank you. I’m here because... well, it’s a little hard to explain, but I just really need some help figuring out what's going on in my head. I work as a computer systems analyst, and I actually finished my graduate degree not too long ago, so I’ve always been someone who relies on logic and fixing things. I used to be so on top of everything. But lately, I just... I can’t shake the feeling that work isn’t safe anymore. \n\nAnd it’s not just about the building or the office, it’s this heavy, constant weight that I’m somehow fundamentally damaged now. I sit at my desk, looking at my monitors and the projects I'm supposed to be managing, and I just feel like the competent, driven person who worked so hard and got promoted just... isn’t really there anymore. It’s like she’s completely gone, and I’m just this broken version trying to pretend I still know how to function. I keep trying to analyze my way out of it—because that’s what I do, I find the flaw in the system and I patch it—but no matter how much I try to reason with myself, I just have this overwhelming belief that I'm ruined and that nothing around me is secure. I don't know if that makes any sense to you, but I just keep spinning my wheels on this, and it's exhausting trying to hide the fact that I feel so entirely different inside.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Well, I know exactly when it started, actually. It’s been... let’s see, about two years and four months now. I can pinpoint the exact timeline because it all traces back to this one specific project we were doing. In my line of work, when we have to push major system updates or do server migrations, we have to do overnight deployments. You know, you have to do it when all the users are offline so you don't disrupt the normal business hours or crash the network while people are trying to work. \n\nSo, I was there late, doing this overnight deployment, just running my scripts and watching the progress bars on my monitors. It was supposed to be quiet, just me and the code, but... that's when the break-in happened. The building was broken into while I was there working. And ever since that specific night, two years and four months ago, this whole feeling just completely latched onto me. I keep looking at the calendar and calculating the time—like, twenty-eight months should be enough time to process an event logically, right? You'd think after more than two years you could just isolate the error, patch the bug, and move on. But ever since that break-in during the deployment, I just... I haven't been able to reset back to the person I was before that night. It's just been this continuous, unbroken loop since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes where you felt an intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes, with symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or shortness of breath?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I know what you're talking about, like a panic attack, right? I've read about those because, obviously, when my brain started glitching after the break-in, I started researching everything. I looked up anxiety, stress responses, the sympathetic nervous system, all of it. I wanted to understand the mechanics of what was happening to me. So I know the checklist—the hyperventilating, the feeling like you're having a heart attack, the sudden peak of terror out of nowhere. But I don't really get those. \n\nIt's not like a sudden spike for me. It's more like... a constant hum in the background. Like a server that's running too hot, all the time. My heart doesn't suddenly start pounding out of my chest, and I don't start gasping for air or sweating through my clothes in the middle of a meeting. It's just this heavy, persistent dread. It's like I'm always waiting for the system to crash, rather than actually experiencing the crash itself, if that makes sense. I don't get those sudden, intense episodes where I feel like I'm dying or losing control in a matter of minutes. It's just a steady state of feeling unsafe.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this sense of being damaged and unsafe only at work, or does it also affect how you feel in other parts of your life, like with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Well, it definitely bleeds over, but it’s most obvious at work because that’s where the metrics are, you know? Like, I can track the decline. It’s not just a feeling; it’s actually impacting my deliverables. My work has slipped enough that I’ve actually missed migration deadlines. And I never used to miss deadlines. I was the person who built the project timelines, so missing them is just... it’s completely unacceptable to me. But I’ve been actively avoiding key late deployments. If a script needs to be run at 2:00 AM, I’ll find an excuse to hand it off, or I’ll try to automate it in a way that’s riskier just so I don’t have to be the one monitoring it late at night. \n\nAnd my boss noticed. I actually got called out in my last performance review, which was devastating. It wasn't just the deadlines, either; they mentioned that I’ve been having conflicts with the team, getting irritable or snapping at people over code reviews or minor server configurations. And that never used to happen. I used to be the one who smoothed things over between the developers and the infrastructure guys. \n\nBut it’s not just the technical work, either. I used to be really involved in our mentorship program, and I was on the organizing committee for these women-in-tech leadership events in the city. I loved doing that, connecting with younger women entering the field, helping them navigate the corporate structure. But I’ve completely pulled back from all of it. I stepped down from the committee and I haven't taken on a new mentee in over a year. I just... I don’t feel steady anymore. I don't feel reliable. How can I sit there and give someone career advice or talk about leadership when I feel like my own internal architecture is collapsing? I just feel like a fraud trying to project this image of a successful, put-together analyst when, underneath it all, I'm dropping the ball on the most basic parts of my job.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid places or situations that remind you of the break-in at your workplace?\n\nPatient: Well, yes. Yes, I do. And it’s actually become this whole... logistical nightmare, to be honest. I mean, my job is literally about optimizing systems and creating efficient workflows, but my own daily routine has become this incredibly convoluted, inefficient process just to avoid anything that triggers that feeling. \n\nFor instance, the building itself. I used to just walk through the main lobby, take the elevator, normal stuff. But now, if I have to be there anywhere near the evening, I absolutely cannot use the main entrance. I’ve started taking this side entrance that goes through the old loading dock area, which is actually less secure logically, but for some reason, walking through that main lobby after dark just... it makes me feel like I’m going to come apart. Like my chest gets tight and I just start visualizing the glass breaking again. \n\nSo I avoid the lobby. But it’s more than just the physical space, it’s the timing of the work itself. Like I mentioned before about the overnight deployments—I’ve been actively trading shifts with the other analysts. I’ll take their early morning database maintenance windows, which nobody wants because you have to log on at 4:30 AM, just so they’ll take my late-night release windows. And if I can’t trade, I’ve started pushing back on the release windows entirely. I’ll argue in the sprint planning meetings that we need to schedule the deployment for a weekend afternoon instead of a weeknight, citing user impact metrics that are, frankly, a bit of a stretch, just to justify not being in that building or even online late at night. \n\nAnd honestly, I’ve just been working from home as much as humanly possible. Our company has a hybrid policy, three days in, two days remote, but I’ve been stretching that to the absolute limit. I’ll claim I have a contractor coming, or I’m feeling slightly under the weather, or I need \"deep focus time\" for a complex script—anything to avoid going into the office, especially if I know I might have to stay past 5:00 PM. Because the second the sun starts going down and the office starts emptying out, that hum I told you about? It gets so loud I can't even look at my monitors. So yeah, I avoid it. I avoid the lobby, I avoid the late shifts, I avoid the building itself whenever I can. It's like I'm constantly rewriting my own schedule just to route around this massive error in my system.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself remembering or reliving the break-in at your workplace?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, I do. And it’s not even like I’m sitting there trying to process the event or think about it on purpose. It’s more like... well, in my line of work, we talk about system interrupts, right? Like a hardware interrupt that just halts the processor and forces it to deal with a high-priority event immediately. That’s exactly what it feels like. It’s entirely involuntary. \n\nIt’s usually triggered by these really specific, otherwise completely mundane sensory inputs. Like... a badge scanner beeping. We have these RFID readers at the office, and they make this very distinct, sharp little chirp when someone badges in. Or, um, heavy footsteps in the hallway. You know how acoustics change when an office is mostly empty? The sound just carries differently, it echoes more. Or even something digital, like a late-night deployment alert pinging on my phone for a server update. \n\nWhen one of those things happens, it doesn't matter where I am or what I'm focusing on. It’s a total system override. I am suddenly put right back in that office on that night. It’s so incredibly visceral. My chest gets really tight, like I physically cannot draw a full breath, and... and then there's this image. The image of the security guard on the floor. It just violently cuts through whatever I’m doing. \n\nI could be mid-sentence in a team stand-up meeting, or deep into writing a complex block of code, or just standing in my kitchen making coffee, and suddenly I’m not there anymore. I’m right back in that moment, seeing him on the floor, hearing the sounds. And it takes me... I don't know, it takes a really long time to kind of reboot and convince my brain that I'm actually safe, that the event isn't happening right this second. It's just so exhausting because I can't control when someone is going to walk heavily down a hall or when a scanner is going to beep, so I'm just constantly vulnerable to my brain forcing me to relive the worst night of my life over and over again.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're observing yourself from outside your body or like you're in a dream when you're reliving the break-in?\n\nPatient: No, I... I don't think so. I mean, I know what you're talking about, I think. I read an article once about depersonalization, how the brain sort of detaches as a defense mechanism during trauma. Like you're watching a movie of yourself. But no, that's not what happens to me at all. It's actually the exact opposite. \n\nWhen I get pulled back into that memory, it's not like I'm floating above it or watching it happen to someone else. I am completely, horribly present in my own body. It's so visceral. Every physical sensation is magnified. The tightness in my chest, the way my heart starts hammering against my ribs, the sweat on my palms... it's all incredibly immediate. I'm not disconnected from it; I'm trapped in it. \n\nI almost wish I could disconnect, honestly. If I could just float away and watch it like a movie, maybe it wouldn't be so exhausting. But instead, it's like my nervous system is firing on all cylinders, screaming that the threat is right in front of me, right now. It's so grounding, but in the worst possible way. It anchors me to that panic. So, no, I don't feel like I'm in a dream or outside myself.
