But being online late, seeing those alerts ping... it’s just too much of a trigger right now, so I make excuses or try to hand them off. \n\nIt’s gotten to the point where my manager actually brought it up in my last performance review. Not just the missed deadlines, but my attitude. Apparently, I’ve been getting into conflicts with my team, snapping at people over code reviews or system architecture disagreements. And that never used to happen. I was always the mediator, the calm one. But now, my fuse is just so short. I feel like I'm constantly running on empty, so any little friction just sparks a massive reaction. \n\nAnd outside of my direct job duties, it’s affecting the things I actually used to care about. I’ve completely pulled back from mentoring the junior analysts. I used to love doing that, helping them navigate the tech stack and the corporate environment. I was also really involved in these women-in-tech leadership events in the city—organizing panels, networking, speaking. But I haven't gone to a single one in months. I just... I don't feel steady anymore. I don't feel reliable. How can I stand up there and talk about leadership and resilience when I can barely get through a Tuesday without feeling like I'm going to jump out of my skin? So, I just isolate. I stay home. It’s safer, but it’s really lonely, and it feels like I’m losing all these parts of my identity that I worked so hard to build.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping at night?\n\nPatient: Sleep... yeah. Yeah, sleep is a whole other issue right now. It's like my brain just refuses to transition into rest mode, no matter how exhausted I am physically. I’ll do all the right things, you know? I’ll turn off all my screens an hour before bed, I’ll read a physical book, I’ll even try those guided meditation apps that everyone swears by, but it just doesn't matter. \n\nMost nights, I just lie there in the dark. And instead of drifting off, my mind starts spinning up all these background processes. I’ll start replaying little details from work, like a comment someone made during a sprint planning meeting, or I’ll obsess over a line of code that wasn’t compiling right. But it always, always circles back to the break-in. \n\nAnd then, when I finally do manage to fall asleep, it’s not even restful. I have these dreams... they’re so vivid. I’ll jolt awake, completely covered in sweat, because in the dream, I’m right back there. I’m hiding under that heavy oak conference table in the boardroom, trying to make myself as small as possible, listening to the sounds of them tearing the office apart outside the door. I can feel the cold floor against my knees, I can smell the stale coffee from the morning meeting... it’s terrifying. I wake up with my heart racing, and then, of course, I can’t get back to sleep for hours. \n\nEven on the rare nights where I manage to string together, say, six or seven hours of uninterrupted sleep, it doesn't feel like I actually slept. I wake up the next morning feeling completely drained. It’s like my system never actually powered down, you know? Like it was just running in a low-power state, constantly scanning for threats all night long. I drag myself out of bed feeling just as exhausted, if not more so, than when I lay down. It’s really taking a toll, because I just never feel recovered. I never feel rested.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to concentrate on things you're trying to do?\n\nPatient: Concentrate? Um... actually, no, I wouldn't say I have trouble concentrating. I mean, I know I just talked about how my focus shatters when I'm dealing with the anxiety and the memories, but that's... that's different. That's me being overwhelmed by a specific stimulus, you know? It's like a system overload, not a hardware failure. \n\nWhen I actually sit down to do a task, if I can get into the zone, my concentration is... well, it's fine. It's the same as it always was. Like, if I'm building a new SQL query or mapping out a network topology, I can still lock in. I can still trace the logic paths and hold all the variables in my head. I don't find myself reading the same paragraph over and over, or forgetting what I was doing in the middle of a task, unless, of course, a memory spikes. \n\nBut just general, day-to-day concentration? No, I don't think that's an issue. I can still follow complex conversations, I can still analyze data sets. It's just that the environment around the task has become so fraught. The actual cognitive ability to focus on the work in front of me, when I'm not actively fighting off a panic response, feels completely intact. It's just the noise around it that's the problem, if that makes sense. So, yeah, the concentration itself isn't broken. It's just... heavily besieged sometimes. But I can still focus when I need to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything related to the break-in at your workplace?\n\nPatient: Guilt... yeah. Yeah, I do. It’s actually something that’s been eating away at me pretty consistently, which is awful because, logically, I know how irrational it sounds. But the logic just doesn't seem to matter when I'm lying awake at night or when my mind starts drifting during the day. \n\nI just keep replaying the whole scenario, specifically regarding the overnight deployment. We were pushing a massive system update, and I was the one managing the timeline. I knew we’d be there late. I knew the building would be mostly empty. And I just keep thinking... I keep thinking that if I had just insisted on better security protocols for that night, or if I had maybe escalated my concerns about the lack of after-hours personnel to the building management beforehand, things would have been different. I mean, we're dealing with sensitive data, we should have had a tighter perimeter anyway. \n\nBut beyond the prep, it's the actual event. When they broke in... I was in the boardroom, like I said, hiding under the table. And I heard them coming down the hall. I heard the commotion. And I had my phone in my hand, but I was just so frozen. I was so terrified that the screen lighting up or the sound of me whispering to a 911 operator would draw them right to me. So I waited. I waited what felt like an eternity before I finally made the call. \n\nAnd the security guard... he was the one who intercepted them first. He got hurt. He got hurt really badly. And I just... I can't shake this thought, this heavy, suffocating thought, that if I had just called sooner, if I had just overcome my own panic for two seconds to dial the police the absolute second I heard the glass shatter, maybe they would have gotten there in time. Maybe the guard wouldn't have been injured. \n\nPart of me—the rational, analytical part of my brain—knows that I was just a civilian in a terrifying situation, that I was hiding for my life, and that I couldn't have predicted what those guys were going to do. But the rest of me just feels entirely responsible. Like my inaction, my delay, directly caused his injuries. It’s like I failed a critical system check, and someone else paid the price for it. So, yes, the guilt is... it's overwhelming sometimes. I just can't stop blaming myself for not doing more, or doing it faster.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: Interest in things... you mean like hobbies and stuff? Um, no, I don't think so. I mean, I know sometimes people get depressed after something like this and just stop caring about everything, but that hasn't really happened to me. I still like the things I used to like. \n\nLike, I'm still really into building my custom mechanical keyboards. I actually just finished a new build last weekend—a 65% layout with a brass plate and these really nice, tactile Holy Panda switches that I had to lube by hand, which takes forever, but the sound profile is just so satisfying when you get it right. I can still spend hours researching different keycap sets or watching typing test videos on YouTube. It's actually one of the few things that kind of forces me to focus on something physical, something right in front of me, instead of getting lost in my head. \n\nAnd I still really enjoy hiking, too. I haven't been out as much lately, mostly just because I've been so exhausted from the lack of sleep we talked about, but the desire to go is definitely still there. I was actually just looking at trail maps for this state park a couple of hours north of here, trying to plan a weekend trip for when the leaves start changing. I love that crisp autumn air and just getting away from all the screens and the noise of the city. \n\nSo, yeah, the actual interest in my hobbies is totally still intact. It's not like the color has drained out of the world or anything. I still want to do things, I still find joy in the things I've always loved. It's just that sometimes the logistics of actually doing them—like finding the energy when I'm running on three hours of terrible sleep, or dealing with the anxiety of going to a crowded trailhead—can be a barrier. But the core interest, the actual enjoyment of the activity itself once I'm doing it? That hasn't gone anywhere. I still very much care about my interests.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your physical health since the break-in?\n\nPatient: Physical health? Um... no, not really. I mean, I know I've talked a lot about the sleep issues and the anxiety, and obviously, those take a physical toll—like, I feel tired, and sometimes my muscles are tense from just being on edge all the time. But as far as actual, distinct changes in my physical health? No, I don't think there's anything new or concerning on that front. \n\nI haven't had any weird aches or pains that I can't explain, no changes in my appetite or digestion, nothing like that. I still try to eat relatively healthy, you know, meal prepping on Sundays so I don't just end up ordering takeout every night when I'm too exhausted to cook. I usually make a big batch of quinoa and roasted vegetables, maybe some grilled chicken. It's just easier that way, especially with my schedule. And I still try to stay somewhat active, even if I haven't been hiking as much as I'd like. I have a standing desk at home, and I try to make sure I'm not just sitting in one position for eight hours straight while I'm working. \n\nI actually had my annual physical with my primary care doctor a couple of weeks ago—well, maybe it was three weeks ago now, time has been a little blurry lately—but anyway, everything checked out fine. My blood pressure was normal, my heart rate was fine, all my blood work came back completely standard. No vitamin deficiencies or anything like that, which I was a little worried about since I haven't been getting out in the sun as much. But nope, all good. \n\nSo, yeah, physically, I seem to be holding up okay. It's really just the mental and emotional stuff that's been thrown completely out of whack since... well, since everything happened. My body itself seems to be functioning just fine, all things considered. I haven't noticed any weird rashes, no hair loss, no sudden weight changes, nothing that would make me think my physical health has deteriorated. It's just... it's just my brain that feels like it's constantly misfiring. But physically? No, I'm okay.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling more irritable or angry than you used to?\n\nPatient: Irritable? Yeah... yeah, I definitely do. It's actually been causing some real friction at work lately, which is so frustrating because I've always prided myself on being really level-headed professionally, you know? I'm an analyst, I'm supposed to be objective. But lately, my fuse is just... it's practically non-existent. \n\nIt happens a lot during our sprint meetings or when we're evaluating peer work. Like, in code reviews I snap at people over small things, like a sloppy rollback plan or someone questioning my timeline, and then I hear myself sounding harsh and personal before I can pull it back. It’s like this immediate, uncontrollable spike of rage. A rollback plan is crucial, obviously, but instead of just pointing out the gaps constructively like I used to, I just go on the offensive. \n\nAnd the worst part is that split second where I'm talking, and I can hear the tone of my own voice—I can hear how aggressive and mean I'm being to a coworker who just asked a simple question about a deadline—but the words are already spilling out. And then I have to sit there and try to backtrack or do damage control, apologize in Slack later, which is just exhausting and embarrassing. \n\nI never used to be like this. I used to be the one who could calmly walk a junior developer through their mistakes without making them feel stupid, or negotiate a deployment schedule without getting defensive. But now, it's like every little question or minor error feels like this massive, irritating obstacle, and I just bite their head off before my rational brain can even catch up. It's like I have absolutely no buffer between feeling mildly annoyed and just completely lashing out at people who really don't deserve it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're detached from yourself or your surroundings?\n\nPatient: Detached? Like... like I'm not really here? Um, no, I don't think I've ever felt anything like that. I mean, I know what you're talking about, I've read about people feeling like they're watching themselves from the outside or like the world around them isn't real, like a movie or something. But no, I haven't experienced that. \n\nIf anything, I feel almost too attached, you know? Like I'm hyper-aware of everything around me, every sound, every movement. It's the opposite of being detached. I'm constantly scanning my environment, constantly feeling every single thing, especially the anxiety and the anger I mentioned. It's all very, very real and immediate to me. \n\nI've never had a moment where I looked at my hands and felt like they weren't mine, or walked down the street and felt like I was in a dream. My surroundings are almost aggressively real to me right now. Like, when I'm in my apartment, I'm so aware of the door, the windows, the sounds in the hallway. I'm definitely not floating away from it all. I'm very much stuck right here in my own head and my own body, for better or worse. So, no, no feelings of detachment or unreality or anything like that. Just... just very grounded, unfortunately grounded, in everything that's going on.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Appetite? Um, no, not really. I mean, I think I mentioned earlier when we were talking about my physical health that my eating habits haven't really changed much. I'm still doing my Sunday meal prep, which honestly has been a lifesaver because by the time I log off work, especially on days when I've had one of those blow-ups in a meeting, I just don't have the energy to stand in the kitchen and chop vegetables or figure out a recipe.

So, no, I'd say my appetite is one of the few things that has stayed completely normal through all of this. It's just the steady, predictable part of my day right now.

Like, in code reviews I snap at people over small things, like a sloppy rollback plan or someone questioning my timeline, and then I hear myself sounding harsh and personal before I can pull it back. It’s like this immediate, uncontrollable spike of rage. A rollback plan is crucial, obviously, but instead of just pointing out the gaps constructively like I used to, I just go on the offensive. \n\nAnd the worst part is that split second where I'm talking, and I can hear the tone of my own voice—I can hear how aggressive and mean I'm being to a coworker who just asked a simple question about a deadline—but the words are already spilling out. And then I have to sit there and try to backtrack or do damage control, apologize in Slack later, which is just exhausting and embarrassing. \n\nI never used to be like this. I used to be the one who could calmly walk a junior developer through their mistakes without making them feel stupid, or negotiate a deployment schedule without getting defensive. But now, it's like every little question or minor error feels like this massive, irritating obstacle, and I just bite their head off before my rational brain can even catch up. It's like I have absolutely no buffer between feeling mildly annoyed and just completely lashing out at people who really don't deserve it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're detached from yourself or your surroundings?\n\nPatient: Detached? Like... like I'm not really here? Um, no, I don't think I've ever felt anything like that. I mean, I know what you're talking about, I've read about people feeling like they're watching themselves from the outside or like the world around them isn't real, like a movie or something. But no, I haven't experienced that. \n\nIf anything, I feel almost too attached, you know? Like I'm hyper-aware of everything around me, every sound, every movement. It's the opposite of being detached. I'm constantly scanning my environment, constantly feeling every single thing, especially the anxiety and the anger I mentioned. It's all very, very real and immediate to me. \n\nI've never had a moment where I looked at my hands and felt like they weren't mine, or walked down the street and felt like I was in a dream. My surroundings are almost aggressively real to me right now. Like, when I'm in my apartment, I'm so aware of the door, the windows, the sounds in the hallway. I'm definitely not floating away from it all. I'm very much stuck right here in my own head and my own body, for better or worse. So, no, no feelings of detachment or unreality or anything like that. Just... just very grounded, unfortunately grounded, in everything that's going on.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite since the break-in at your workplace?\n\nPatient: Appetite? Um, no, not really. I mean, I think I mentioned earlier when we were talking about my physical health that my eating habits haven't really changed much. I'm still doing my Sunday meal prep, which honestly has been a lifesaver because by the time I log off work, especially on days when I've had one of those blow-ups in a meeting, I just don't have the energy to stand in the kitchen and chop vegetables or figure out a recipe. \n\nSo yeah, I still eat the same things. I still have my usual coffee and oatmeal in the morning, usually while I'm reviewing the overnight logs. And then lunch is whatever I prepped—like I said, usually quinoa, some roasted broccoli or Brussels sprouts, maybe some chicken or tofu. I try to keep it balanced. I haven't noticed myself skipping meals or feeling too nauseous to eat, which I know can happen when you're stressed. And on the flip side, I haven't been stress-eating either. I haven't been, you know, plowing through bags of chips or ordering a pizza every night just for comfort. \n\nMy stomach feels fine, my digestion is normal. It's funny, actually, because you'd think with how wired and anxious I feel all the time, my stomach would be in knots, but it really hasn't affected my ability to eat. I still get hungry around the same times I always did, usually around 12:30 for lunch and then 7:00 for dinner. Sometimes I'll have an apple or a handful of almonds in the afternoon if I'm dragging, but that's always been my routine. So, no, I'd say my appetite is one of the few things that has stayed completely normal through all of this. It's just the steady, predictable part of my day right now."
  }
